<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1033289096">.mw-parser-output .hatnote{font-style:italic}.mw-parser-output div.hatnote{padding-left:1.6em;margin-bottom:0.5em}.mw-parser-output .hatnote i{font-style:normal}.mw-parser-output .hatnote+link+.hatnote{margin-top:-0.5em}</style><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">This article is about the biblical figure. For other uses, see <a href="/wiki/Mary_Magdalene_(disambiguation)" class="mw-disambig" title="Mary Magdalene (disambiguation)">Mary Magdalene (disambiguation)</a>.</div>
<p class="mw-empty-elt">

</p>

<p><b>Mary Magdalene</b><sup id="cite_ref-pronounce_1-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-pronounce-1">&#91;a&#93;</a></sup> (sometimes called <b>Mary of Magdala</b>, or simply the <b>Magdalene</b> or the <b>Madeleine</b>) was a woman who, according to the four <a href="/wiki/Canonical_gospel" class="mw-redirect" title="Canonical gospel">canonical gospels</a>, traveled with <a href="/wiki/Jesus" title="Jesus">Jesus</a> as one of his followers and was a witness to <a href="/wiki/Crucifixion_of_Jesus" title="Crucifixion of Jesus">his crucifixion</a> and 
<a href="/wiki/Resurrection_of_Jesus" title="Resurrection of Jesus">resurrection</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-BBC_2-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-BBC-2">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> She is mentioned by name twelve times in the canonical gospels, more than most of the <a href="/wiki/Apostles_in_the_New_Testament" title="Apostles in the New Testament">apostles</a> and more than any other woman in the gospels, other than Jesus' family. Mary's <a href="/wiki/Epithet" title="Epithet">epithet</a> <i>Magdalene</i> may mean that she came from the town of <a href="/wiki/Magdala" title="Magdala">Magdala</a>, a fishing town on the western shore of the <a href="/wiki/Sea_of_Galilee" title="Sea of Galilee">Sea of Galilee</a> in <a href="/wiki/Roman_Judea" class="mw-redirect" title="Roman Judea">Roman Judea</a>.
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Gospel_of_Luke" title="Gospel of Luke">Gospel of Luke</a> <a href="/wiki/Luke_8" title="Luke 8">chapter 8</a> lists Mary Magdalene as one of the women who traveled with Jesus and helped support his ministry "out of their resources", indicating that she was probably wealthy. The same passage also states that seven demons <a href="/wiki/Exorcism" title="Exorcism">had been driven out of her</a>, a statement which is repeated in <a href="/wiki/Mark_16" title="Mark 16">Mark 16</a>. In all the four canonical gospels, Mary Magdalene was a witness to the crucifixion of Jesus and, in the <a href="/wiki/Synoptic_Gospels" title="Synoptic Gospels">Synoptic Gospels</a>, she was also present at his burial. All the four gospels identified her, either alone or as a member of a larger <a href="/wiki/The_Three_Marys" title="The Three Marys">group of women</a> which includes <a href="/wiki/Mary,_mother_of_Jesus" title="Mary, mother of Jesus">Jesus' mother</a>, as the first to witness the <a href="/wiki/Empty_tomb" title="Empty tomb">empty tomb</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-BBC_2-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-BBC-2">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> and, either alone or as a member of a group, as the first to witness Jesus' resurrection.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEThompson1995_3-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEThompson1995-3">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>For these reasons, Mary Magdalene is known in some Christian traditions as the "apostle to the apostles". She is a central figure in later <a href="/wiki/Gnosticism" title="Gnosticism">Gnostic</a> Christian writings, including the <a href="/wiki/Dialogue_of_the_Saviour" title="Dialogue of the Saviour">Dialogue of the Savior</a>, the <i><a href="/wiki/Pistis_Sophia" title="Pistis Sophia">Pistis Sophia</a></i>, the <a href="/wiki/Gospel_of_Thomas" title="Gospel of Thomas">Gospel of Thomas</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Gospel_of_Philip" title="Gospel of Philip">Gospel of Philip</a>, and the <a href="/wiki/Gospel_of_Mary" title="Gospel of Mary">Gospel of Mary</a>. These texts portray her as an apostle, as Jesus' closest and most beloved disciple and the only one who truly understood his teachings. In the <a href="/wiki/Gnostic_texts" class="mw-redirect" title="Gnostic texts">Gnostic texts</a>, or Gnostic gospels, Mary's closeness to Jesus results in tension with another disciple, <a href="/wiki/Saint_Peter" title="Saint Peter">Peter</a>, due to her sex and Peter's envy of the special teachings given to her. In the <a href="/wiki/Gospel_of_Philip" title="Gospel of Philip">Gospel of Philip</a>'s text she is described as Jesus' companion, as the disciple Jesus loved the most and the one Jesus kissed on the mouth,<sup id="cite_ref-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-4">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> which has led some people to conclude that she and Jesus were in a relationship. Some fiction portrays her as the wife of Jesus.
</p><p>The portrayal of Mary Magdalene as a prostitute began in 591, when <a href="/wiki/Pope_Gregory_I" title="Pope Gregory I">Pope Gregory I</a> conflated Mary Magdalene, who was introduced in Luke 8:2, with <a href="/wiki/Mary_of_Bethany" title="Mary of Bethany">Mary of Bethany</a> (Luke 10:39) and the unnamed "sinful woman" who <a href="/wiki/Anointing_of_Jesus" title="Anointing of Jesus">anointed Jesus' feet</a> in Luke 7:36–50. Pope Gregory's Easter sermon resulted in a widespread belief that Mary Magdalene was a repentant prostitute or promiscuous woman.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMeyers2000122Named_Women:_Mary_3_(Magdalene)_5-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMeyers2000122Named_Women:_Mary_3_(Magdalene)-5">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-BBC_2-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-BBC-2">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> Elaborate medieval legends from Western Europe then emerged, which told exaggerated tales of Mary Magdalene's wealth and beauty, as well as of her alleged journey to southern Gaul (modern-day France). The identification of Mary Magdalene with Mary of Bethany and the unnamed "sinful woman" was still a major controversy in the years leading up to the <a href="/wiki/Reformation" title="Reformation">Reformation</a>, and some <a href="/wiki/Protestantism" title="Protestantism">Protestant</a> leaders rejected it. During the <a href="/wiki/Counter-Reformation" title="Counter-Reformation">Counter-Reformation</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Catholic_Church" title="Catholic Church">Catholic Church</a> emphasized Mary Magdalene as a symbol of <a href="/wiki/Penance" title="Penance">penance</a>. In 1969, <a href="/wiki/Pope_Paul_VI" title="Pope Paul VI">Pope Paul VI</a> removed the identification of Mary Magdalene with Mary of Bethany and the "sinful woman" from the <a href="/wiki/General_Roman_Calendar" title="General Roman Calendar">General Roman Calendar</a>, but the view of her as a former prostitute has persisted in popular culture.
</p><p>Mary Magdalene is considered to be a <a href="/wiki/Saint" title="Saint">saint</a> by the <a href="/wiki/Catholic_Church" title="Catholic Church">Catholic</a>, <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox_Church" title="Eastern Orthodox Church">Eastern Orthodox</a>, <a href="/wiki/Anglican_Communion" title="Anglican Communion">Anglican</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Lutheranism" title="Lutheranism">Lutheran</a> denominations. In 2016, <a href="/wiki/Pope_Francis" title="Pope Francis">Pope Francis</a> raised the level of liturgical memory on July 22 from memorial to <a href="/wiki/Calendar_of_saints" title="Calendar of saints">feast</a>, and for her to be referred to as the "Apostle of the apostles".<sup id="cite_ref-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-6">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup> Other Protestant churches honor her as a heroine of the faith. The Eastern Orthodox churches also commemorate her on the Sunday of the <a href="/wiki/Myrrhbearers" title="Myrrhbearers">Myrrhbearers</a>, the Orthodox equivalent of one of the Western <a href="/wiki/Three_Marys" class="mw-redirect" title="Three Marys">Three Marys</a> traditions.
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Life">Life</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Mary_Magdalene&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Life">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>It is widely accepted among secular historians that, like Jesus, Mary Magdalene was a real historical figure.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECasey2010475_7-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECasey2010475-7">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMaisch19989_8-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMaisch19989-8">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006185–187_9-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006185–187-9">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> Nonetheless, very little is known about her life.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006185–187,_247_10-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006185–187,_247-10">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup> Unlike <a href="/wiki/Paul_the_Apostle" title="Paul the Apostle">Paul the Apostle</a>, Mary Magdalene left behind no known writings of her own.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006247_11-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006247-11">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> She was never mentioned in any of the <a href="/wiki/Pauline_epistles" title="Pauline epistles">Pauline epistles</a> or in any of the <a href="/wiki/Catholic_epistles" title="Catholic epistles">general epistles</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006185_12-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006185-12">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHaag2016152_13-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHaag2016152-13">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> The earliest and most reliable sources about her life are the three <a href="/wiki/Synoptic_Gospels" title="Synoptic Gospels">Synoptic Gospels</a> of <a href="/wiki/Gospel_of_Mark" title="Gospel of Mark">Mark</a>, <a href="/wiki/Gospel_of_Matthew" title="Gospel of Matthew">Matthew</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Gospel_of_Luke" title="Gospel of Luke">Luke</a>, which were all written during the first century AD.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECasey2010543–544_14-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECasey2010543–544-14">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006185–187,_218_15-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006185–187,_218-15">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHurtado2005267,_355_16-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHurtado2005267,_355-16">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="During_Jesus.27_ministry"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="During_Jesus'_ministry">During Jesus' ministry</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Mary_Magdalene&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: During Jesus&#039; ministry">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Magdala_um_1900.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8b/Magdala_um_1900.jpg/220px-Magdala_um_1900.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="159" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8b/Magdala_um_1900.jpg/330px-Magdala_um_1900.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8b/Magdala_um_1900.jpg/440px-Magdala_um_1900.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3421" data-file-height="2469" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Magdala_um_1900.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Photograph taken <abbr title="circa">c.</abbr> 1900 of <a href="/wiki/Al-Majdal,_Tiberias" title="Al-Majdal, Tiberias">al-Majdal</a>, a village standing among the ruins of <a href="/wiki/Magdala" title="Magdala">Magdala</a>, Mary Magdalene's hometown<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECasey2010193_17-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECasey2010193-17">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006197_18-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006197-18">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMaisch19982_19-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMaisch19982-19">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup></div></div></div>
<p>Mary Magdalene's <a href="/wiki/Epithet" title="Epithet">epithet</a> <i>Magdalene</i> (<span title="Ancient Greek (to 1453)-language text"><span lang="grc">ἡ Μαγδαληνή</span></span>; literally "the Magdalene") most likely means that she came from <a href="/wiki/Magdala" title="Magdala">Magdala</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECasey2010193_17-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECasey2010193-17">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006197_18-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006197-18">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Migdal_20-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Migdal-20">&#91;b&#93;</a></sup> a village on the western shore of the <a href="/wiki/Sea_of_Galilee" title="Sea of Galilee">Sea of Galilee</a> that was primarily known in antiquity as a fishing town.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECasey2010193_17-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECasey2010193-17">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006197–198_21-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006197–198-21">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMaisch19982–3_22-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMaisch19982–3-22">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> <i>Mary</i> was, by far, the most common Jewish <a href="/wiki/Given_name" title="Given name">given name</a> for girls and women during the first century,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECasey2010193_17-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECasey2010193-17">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-MMname_23-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-MMname-23">&#91;c&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-greek3137_24-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-greek3137-24">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> with nearly a fourth of women bearing some variant of the name,<sup id="cite_ref-25" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-25">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> so it was necessary for the authors of the gospels to call her <i>Magdalene</i> in order to distinguish her from the other women named Mary who followed Jesus.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECasey2010193_17-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECasey2010193-17">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup> Although the <a href="/wiki/Gospel_of_Mark" title="Gospel of Mark">Gospel of Mark</a>, reputed by scholars to be the earliest surviving gospel, does not mention Mary Magdalene until Jesus' crucifixion,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECasey2010194_26-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECasey2010194-26">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup> the <a href="/wiki/Gospel_of_Luke" title="Gospel of Luke">Gospel of Luke</a> 8:2–3<sup id="cite_ref-27" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-27">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup> provides a brief summary of her role during his ministry:<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECasey2010192_28-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECasey2010192-28">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r996844942">.mw-parser-output .templatequote{overflow:hidden;margin:1em 0;padding:0 40px}.mw-parser-output .templatequote .templatequotecite{line-height:1.5em;text-align:left;padding-left:1.6em;margin-top:0}</style><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>Soon afterwards he went on through cities and villages, proclaiming and bringing the good news of the <a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_God" class="mw-redirect" title="Kingdom of God">kingdom of God</a>. The twelve were with him, as well as some women who had been cured of evil spirits and infirmities: Mary, called Magdalene, from whom seven demons had gone out, and <a href="/wiki/Joanna,_wife_of_Chuza" title="Joanna, wife of Chuza">Joanna</a>, the wife of <a href="/wiki/Herod_Antipas" title="Herod Antipas">Herod</a>'s steward Chuza, and <a href="/wiki/Susanna_(disciple)" title="Susanna (disciple)">Susanna</a>, and many others, who provided for them out of their resources.</p><div class="templatequotecite">—&#8201;<cite>Luke 8:1-3<sup id="cite_ref-29" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-29">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup></cite></div></blockquote>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:262px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Paolo_Veronese,_The_Conversion_of_Mary_Magdalene.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cc/Paolo_Veronese%2C_The_Conversion_of_Mary_Magdalene.jpg/260px-Paolo_Veronese%2C_The_Conversion_of_Mary_Magdalene.jpg" decoding="async" width="260" height="187" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cc/Paolo_Veronese%2C_The_Conversion_of_Mary_Magdalene.jpg/390px-Paolo_Veronese%2C_The_Conversion_of_Mary_Magdalene.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cc/Paolo_Veronese%2C_The_Conversion_of_Mary_Magdalene.jpg/520px-Paolo_Veronese%2C_The_Conversion_of_Mary_Magdalene.jpg 2x" data-file-width="6000" data-file-height="4315" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Paolo_Veronese,_The_Conversion_of_Mary_Magdalene.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><i><a href="/wiki/The_Conversion_of_Mary_Magdalene" title="The Conversion of Mary Magdalene">The Conversion of Mary Magdalene</a></i> (<abbr title="circa">c.</abbr> 1548) by <a href="/wiki/Paolo_Veronese" title="Paolo Veronese">Paolo Veronese</a></div></div></div>
<p>According to <a href="/wiki/Gospel_of_Luke" title="Gospel of Luke">Gospel of Luke</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-30" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-30">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup> Jesus exorcised "seven demons" from Mary Magdalene.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECasey2010192–193_31-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECasey2010192–193-31">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006206–207_32-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006206–207-32">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEChilton200525–28_33-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChilton200525–28-33">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup> That seven demons had possessed Mary is repeated in Mark 16:9,<sup id="cite_ref-34" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-34">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006207_35-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006207-35">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup> part of the <a href="/wiki/Gospel_of_Mark#The_ending_of_the_gospel_of_Mark" title="Gospel of Mark">"longer ending"</a> of that gospel – this is not found in the earliest manuscripts and is actually a second-century addition to the original text, possibly based on the Gospel of Luke.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006207_35-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006207-35">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMayMetzger1977_36-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMayMetzger1977-36">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup> In the first century, demons were believed widely to cause physical and psychological illness.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKelly200695_37-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKelly200695-37">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECasey2010192–193_31-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECasey2010192–193-31">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006206–207_32-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006206–207-32">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Bruce_Chilton" title="Bruce Chilton">Bruce Chilton</a>, a scholar of early Christianity, states that the reference to the number of demons being "seven" may mean that Mary had to undergo seven exorcisms, probably over a long period of time, due to the first six being partially or wholly unsuccessful.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEChilton200525–28_33-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChilton200525–28-33">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Bart_D._Ehrman" title="Bart D. Ehrman">Bart D. Ehrman</a>, a New Testament scholar and historian of early Christianity, contends that the number seven may be merely symbolic,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006206–207_32-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006206–207-32">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> since, in Jewish tradition, seven was the number of completion,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006206–207_32-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006206–207-32">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> so that Mary was possessed by seven demons may simply mean she was completely overwhelmed by their power.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006206–207_32-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006206–207-32">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> In either case, Mary must have suffered from severe emotional or psychological trauma for an exorcism of this kind to have been perceived as necessary.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECasey2010192–193_31-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECasey2010192–193-31">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006206–207_32-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006206–207-32">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> Consequently, her devotion to Jesus resulting from this healing must have been very strong.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECasey2010193_17-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECasey2010193-17">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEChilton200528–30_38-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChilton200528–30-38">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESchaberg200479–80_39-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESchaberg200479–80-39">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup> The Gospels’ writers normally relish giving dramatic descriptions of Jesus' public exorcisms, with the possessed person wailing, thrashing, and tearing his or her clothes in front of a crowd.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEChilton200526_40-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChilton200526-40">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> By contrast, that Mary's exorcism receives little attention may indicate that either Jesus performed it privately  or that the recorders did not perceive it as particularly dramatic.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEChilton200526_40-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChilton200526-40">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Since Mary is listed as one of the women who supported Jesus' ministry financially, she must have been relatively wealthy.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECasey2010193_17-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECasey2010193-17">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006195,_198_41-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006195,_198-41">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup> The places where she and the other women are mentioned throughout the gospels indicate strongly that they were vital to Jesus' ministry<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECasey2010192–195_42-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECasey2010192–195-42">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006196_43-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006196-43">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESanders1993124–125_44-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESanders1993124–125-44">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHaag2016_45-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHaag2016-45">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup> and that Mary Magdalene always appears first, whenever she is listed in the Synoptic Gospels as a member of a <a href="/wiki/The_Three_Marys" title="The Three Marys">group of women</a>, indicates that she was seen as the most important out of all of them.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECasey2010194–195_46-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECasey2010194–195-46">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESanders1993124_47-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESanders1993124-47">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERicci199471,_127,_139_48-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERicci199471,_127,_139-48">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup> Carla Ricci notes that, in lists of the disciples, Mary Magdalene occupies a similar position among Jesus' female followers as Simon Peter does among the male apostles.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERicci199471,_127,_139_48-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERicci199471,_127,_139-48">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>That women played such an active and important role in Jesus' ministry was not entirely radical or even unique;<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006196_43-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006196-43">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHaag2016_45-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHaag2016-45">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup> inscriptions from a synagogue in <a href="/wiki/Aphrodisias" title="Aphrodisias">Aphrodisias</a> in <a href="/wiki/Asia_Minor" class="mw-redirect" title="Asia Minor">Asia Minor</a> from around the same time period reveal that many of the major donors to the synagogue were women.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006196_43-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006196-43">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> Jesus' ministry did bring women greater liberation than they would typically have held in mainstream Jewish society.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006195–196_49-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006195–196-49">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHaag2016_45-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHaag2016-45">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Witness_to_Jesus.27_crucifixion_and_burial"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Witness_to_Jesus'_crucifixion_and_burial">Witness to Jesus' crucifixion and burial</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Mary_Magdalene&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: Witness to Jesus&#039; crucifixion and burial">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Weyden,_Rogier_van_der_-_Descent_from_the_Cross_-_Detail_Mary_Magdalene.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/48/Weyden%2C_Rogier_van_der_-_Descent_from_the_Cross_-_Detail_Mary_Magdalene.jpg/220px-Weyden%2C_Rogier_van_der_-_Descent_from_the_Cross_-_Detail_Mary_Magdalene.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="264" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/48/Weyden%2C_Rogier_van_der_-_Descent_from_the_Cross_-_Detail_Mary_Magdalene.jpg/330px-Weyden%2C_Rogier_van_der_-_Descent_from_the_Cross_-_Detail_Mary_Magdalene.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/48/Weyden%2C_Rogier_van_der_-_Descent_from_the_Cross_-_Detail_Mary_Magdalene.jpg/440px-Weyden%2C_Rogier_van_der_-_Descent_from_the_Cross_-_Detail_Mary_Magdalene.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2000" data-file-height="2401" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Weyden,_Rogier_van_der_-_Descent_from_the_Cross_-_Detail_Mary_Magdalene.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Detail of Mary Magdalene weeping at the crucifixion of Jesus, as portrayed in <i><a href="/wiki/The_Descent_from_the_Cross_(van_der_Weyden)" title="The Descent from the Cross (van der Weyden)">The Descent from the Cross</a></i> (<abbr title="circa">c.</abbr> 1435) by the Flemish artist <a href="/wiki/Rogier_van_der_Weyden" title="Rogier van der Weyden">Rogier van der Weyden</a><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECampbell20092–64_50-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECampbell20092–64-50">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup></div></div></div>
<p>All four canonical gospels agree that several other women watched Jesus' crucifixion from a distance, with three explicitly naming Mary Magdalene as present.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006223_51-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006223-51">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup> <a href="https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Bible_(King_James)/Mark#15:40" class="extiw" title="s:Bible (King James)/Mark">Mark 15:40</a> lists the names of these women as Mary Magdalene; <a href="/wiki/Mary,_mother_of_James" title="Mary, mother of James">Mary, mother of James</a>; and <a href="/wiki/Salome_(disciple)" title="Salome (disciple)">Salome</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006223_51-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006223-51">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup> <a href="https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Bible_(King_James)/Matthew#27:55" class="extiw" title="s:Bible (King James)/Matthew">Matthew 27:55–56</a> lists Mary Magdalene, Mary mother of James and Joseph, and the unnamed mother of the sons of <a href="/wiki/Zebedee" title="Zebedee">Zebedee</a> (who may be the same person Mark calls Salome).<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006223_51-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006223-51">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup> <a href="https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Bible_(King_James)/Luke#23:49" class="extiw" title="s:Bible (King James)/Luke">Luke 23:49</a> mentioned a group of women watching the crucifixion, but did not give any of their names.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006223_51-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006223-51">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup> <a href="https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Bible_(King_James)/John#19:25" class="extiw" title="s:Bible (King James)/John">John 19:25</a> lists <a href="/wiki/Mary,_mother_of_Jesus" title="Mary, mother of Jesus">Mary, mother of Jesus</a>, her sister, <a href="/wiki/Mary_of_Clopas" title="Mary of Clopas">Mary, wife of Clopas</a>, and Mary Magdalene as witnesses to the crucifixion.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006223_51-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006223-51">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Virtually all reputable historians agree that Jesus was crucified by the Romans under the orders of <a href="/wiki/Pontius_Pilate" title="Pontius Pilate">Pontius Pilate</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHerzog20051–6_52-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHerzog20051–6-52">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPowell1998168_53-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPowell1998168-53">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECrossan1995145_54-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECrossan1995145-54">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELevineAllisonCrossan20064_55-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELevineAllisonCrossan20064-55">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup> James Dunn states of baptism and crucifixion that these "two facts in the life of Jesus command almost universal assent".<sup id="cite_ref-56" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-56">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup> Nonetheless, the gospels' accounts of Jesus' crucifixion differ considerably<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006217–223_57-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006217–223-57">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup> and most secular historians agree that some of the details in the accounts have been altered to fit their authors' theological agendas.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006217–223_57-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006217–223-57">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup> Ehrman states that the presence of Mary Magdalene and the other women at the cross is probably historical because Christians would have been unlikely to make up that the main witnesses to the crucifixion were women<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006225–226_58-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006225–226-58">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup> and also because their presence is attested in both the Synoptic Gospels and in the <a href="/wiki/Gospel_of_John" title="Gospel of John">Gospel of John</a> independently. <sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006226_59-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006226-59">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Maurice_Casey" title="Maurice Casey">Maurice Casey</a> concurs that the presence of Mary Magdalene and the other women at the crucifixion of Jesus may be recorded as an historical fact.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECasey2010475_7-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECasey2010475-7">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> According to <a href="/wiki/E._P._Sanders" title="E. P. Sanders">E. P. Sanders</a>, the reason why the women watched the crucifixion even after the male disciples had fled may have been because they were less likely to be arrested, they were braver than the men, or some combination thereof.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESanders1993276_60-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESanders1993276-60">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:242px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Raffaello,_pala_baglioni,_deposizione.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f2/Raffaello%2C_pala_baglioni%2C_deposizione.jpg/240px-Raffaello%2C_pala_baglioni%2C_deposizione.jpg" decoding="async" width="240" height="248" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f2/Raffaello%2C_pala_baglioni%2C_deposizione.jpg/360px-Raffaello%2C_pala_baglioni%2C_deposizione.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f2/Raffaello%2C_pala_baglioni%2C_deposizione.jpg/480px-Raffaello%2C_pala_baglioni%2C_deposizione.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2000" data-file-height="2068" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Raffaello,_pala_baglioni,_deposizione.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><i><a href="/wiki/The_Deposition_(Raphael)" title="The Deposition (Raphael)">The Deposition</a></i> (1507) by <a href="/wiki/Raphael" title="Raphael">Raphael</a>, showing a distressed, reddish-blond-haired Mary Magdalene dressed in fine clothes clutching the hand of Jesus' body as he is carried to the tomb<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEJonesPenny198346–47_61-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEJonesPenny198346–47-61">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup></div></div></div>
<p>All four canonical gospels, as well as the apocryphal <a href="/wiki/Gospel_of_Peter" title="Gospel of Peter">Gospel of Peter</a>, agree that Jesus' body was taken down from the cross and buried by a man named <a href="/wiki/Joseph_of_Arimathea" title="Joseph of Arimathea">Joseph of Arimathea</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006223_51-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006223-51">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup> <a href="https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Bible_(King_James)/Mark#15:47" class="extiw" title="s:Bible (King James)/Mark">Mark 15:47</a> lists Mary Magdalene and Mary, mother of Jesus as witnesses to the burial of Jesus.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006223_51-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006223-51">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup> <a href="https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Bible_(King_James)/Matthew#27:61" class="extiw" title="s:Bible (King James)/Matthew">Matthew 27:61</a> lists Mary Magdalene and "the other Mary" as witnesses.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006223_51-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006223-51">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup> <a href="https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Bible_(King_James)/Luke#23:55" class="extiw" title="s:Bible (King James)/Luke">Luke 23:55</a> mentions "the women who had followed him from Galilee", but does not list any of their names.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006223_51-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006223-51">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup> <a href="https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Bible_(King_James)/John#19:39" class="extiw" title="s:Bible (King James)/John">John 19:39–42</a> does not mention any women present during Joseph's burial of Jesus,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006223_51-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006223-51">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup> but does mention the presence of <a href="/wiki/Nicodemus" title="Nicodemus">Nicodemus</a>, a Pharisee with whom Jesus had a conversation near the beginning of the gospel.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006223_51-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006223-51">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup> Ehrman, who previously accepted the story of Jesus' burial as historical, now rejects it as a later invention on the basis that Roman governors almost never allowed for executed criminals to be given any kind of burial<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2014151–161_62-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2014151–161-62">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup> and Pontius Pilate in particular was not "the sort of ruler who would break with tradition and policy when kindly asked by a member of the Jewish council to provide a decent burial for a crucified victim."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2014163_63-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2014163-63">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Post-modernist <a href="/wiki/John_Dominic_Crossan" title="John Dominic Crossan">John Dominic Crossan</a> has argued that Jesus' body was probably eaten by wild dogs.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2014163_63-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2014163-63">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECasey2010448_64-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECasey2010448-64">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup> Ehrman notes that this was the most common fate for victims of crucifixion<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2014156–164_65-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2014156–164-65">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup> but states that it is impossible to know for certain what happened to Jesus' body once it was removed from the cross.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2014156–169_66-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2014156–169-66">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup> Casey argues that Jesus was given a proper burial by Joseph of Arimathea,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECasey2010448–453_67-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECasey2010448–453-67">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup> noting that, on some very rare occasions, Roman governors did release the bodies of executed prisoners for burial. <sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECasey2010449–450_68-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECasey2010449–450-68">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup> Nonetheless, he rejects that Jesus could have been interred in an expensive tomb with a stone rolled in front of it like the one described in the gospels,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECasey2010449–453_69-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECasey2010449–453-69">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup> leading him to conclude that Mary and the other women must not have seen the tomb.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECasey2010449–453_69-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECasey2010449–453-69">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup> Sanders affirms Jesus' burial by Joseph of Arimathea in the presence of Mary Magdalene and the other female followers as completely historical.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESanders1993274–276_70-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESanders1993274–276-70">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Resurrection_of_Jesus">Resurrection of Jesus</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Mary_Magdalene&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: Resurrection of Jesus">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:242px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Annibale_Carracci_-_Holy_Women_at_Christ%27_s_Tomb_-_WGA4454.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/83/Annibale_Carracci_-_Holy_Women_at_Christ%27_s_Tomb_-_WGA4454.jpg/240px-Annibale_Carracci_-_Holy_Women_at_Christ%27_s_Tomb_-_WGA4454.jpg" decoding="async" width="240" height="199" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/83/Annibale_Carracci_-_Holy_Women_at_Christ%27_s_Tomb_-_WGA4454.jpg/360px-Annibale_Carracci_-_Holy_Women_at_Christ%27_s_Tomb_-_WGA4454.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/83/Annibale_Carracci_-_Holy_Women_at_Christ%27_s_Tomb_-_WGA4454.jpg/480px-Annibale_Carracci_-_Holy_Women_at_Christ%27_s_Tomb_-_WGA4454.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3613" data-file-height="2998" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Annibale_Carracci_-_Holy_Women_at_Christ%27_s_Tomb_-_WGA4454.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><i>Holy Women at Christ's Tomb</i> (<abbr title="circa">c.</abbr> 1590s) by <a href="/wiki/Annibale_Carracci" title="Annibale Carracci">Annibale Carracci</a>. In <a href="https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Bible_(King_James)/Matthew#28:1" class="extiw" title="s:Bible (King James)/Matthew">Matthew 28:1–10</a>, Mary Magdalene and "the other Mary" encounter an angel at the tomb, who tells them that Christ has risen.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECasey2010462_71-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECasey2010462-71">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006227–229_72-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006227–229-72">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESanders1993276–280_73-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESanders1993276–280-73">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup></div></div></div>
<p>The earliest description of <a href="/wiki/Post-resurrection_appearances_of_Jesus" class="mw-redirect" title="Post-resurrection appearances of Jesus">Jesus' post-resurrection appearances</a> is a quotation of a <a href="/wiki/Pre-Pauline_Christianity" class="mw-redirect" title="Pre-Pauline Christianity">pre-Pauline</a> creed preserved by Paul the Apostle in <a href="https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Bible_(King_James)/1_Corinthians#15:3" class="extiw" title="s:Bible (King James)/1 Corinthians">1 Corinthians 15:3–8</a>, which was written roughly 20 years before any of the gospels.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2014137–143_74-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2014137–143-74">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup> This passage made no mention of Mary Magdalene, the other women, or the story of the empty tomb at all,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECasey2010456–457_75-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECasey2010456–457-75">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2014142–143_76-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2014142–143-76">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup> but rather credits Simon Peter with having been the first to see the risen Jesus.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECasey2010456–457_75-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECasey2010456–457-75">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESanders1993277_77-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESanders1993277-77">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2014137–140_78-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2014137–140-78">&#91;75&#93;</a></sup> Despite this, all four canonical gospels, as well as the apocryphal Gospel of Peter, agreed that Mary Magdalene, either alone or as a member of a group, was the first person to discover that Jesus' tomb was empty.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006226_59-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006226-59">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHinkle2003446_79-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHinkle2003446-79">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup> Nonetheless, the details of the accounts differ drastically.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006227–229_72-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006227–229-72">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>According to <a href="https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Bible_(King_James)/Mark#16:1" class="extiw" title="s:Bible (King James)/Mark">Mark 16:1–8</a>, the earliest account of the discovery of the empty tomb, Mary Magdalene, Mary the mother of James, and Salome went to the tomb just after sunrise, a day and half after Jesus' burial and found that the stone had already been rolled away.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006227–229_72-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006227–229-72">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESanders1993276–280_73-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESanders1993276–280-73">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECasey2010461–462_80-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECasey2010461–462-80">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup> They went inside and saw a young man dressed in white, who told them that Jesus had risen from the dead and instructed them to tell the male disciples that he would meet them in Galilee.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECasey2010462_71-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECasey2010462-71">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006227–229_72-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006227–229-72">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESanders1993276–280_73-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESanders1993276–280-73">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup> Instead, the women ran away and told no one, because they were too afraid.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECasey2010462_71-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECasey2010462-71">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006227–229_72-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006227–229-72">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESanders1993276–280_73-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESanders1993276–280-73">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup> The original text of the gospel ends here, without the resurrected Jesus ever actually making an appearance to anyone.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECasey2010462_71-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECasey2010462-71">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESanders1993276–280_73-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESanders1993276–280-73">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006228_81-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006228-81">&#91;78&#93;</a></sup> Casey argues that the reason for this abrupt ending may be because the Gospel of Mark is an unfinished first draft.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECasey2010462_71-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECasey2010462-71">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>According to <a href="https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Bible_(King_James)/Matthew#28:1" class="extiw" title="s:Bible (King James)/Matthew">Matthew 28:1–10</a>, Mary Magdalene and "the other Mary" went to the tomb.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECasey2010462_71-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECasey2010462-71">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006227–229_72-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006227–229-72">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESanders1993276–280_73-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESanders1993276–280-73">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup> An earthquake occurred and an angel dressed in white descended from Heaven and rolled aside the stone as the women were watching.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECasey2010462_71-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECasey2010462-71">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006227–229_72-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006227–229-72">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESanders1993276–280_73-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESanders1993276–280-73">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup> The angel told them that Jesus had risen from the dead.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006227–229_72-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006227–229-72">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESanders1993276–280_73-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESanders1993276–280-73">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECasey2010462_71-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECasey2010462-71">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup> Then the risen Jesus himself appeared to the women as they were leaving the tomb and told them to tell the other disciples that he would meet them in Galilee.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECasey2010462_71-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECasey2010462-71">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006227–229_72-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006227–229-72">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESanders1993276–280_73-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESanders1993276–280-73">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup> According to <a href="https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Bible_(King_James)/Luke#24:1" class="extiw" title="s:Bible (King James)/Luke">Luke 24:1–12</a> a group of unnamed women went to the tomb and found the stone already rolled away, as in Mark.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006227–229_72-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006227–229-72">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESanders1993276–280_73-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESanders1993276–280-73">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECasey2010463_82-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECasey2010463-82">&#91;79&#93;</a></sup> They went inside and saw two young men dressed in white who told them that Jesus had risen from the dead.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006227–229_72-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006227–229-72">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESanders1993276–280_73-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESanders1993276–280-73">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECasey2010463_82-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECasey2010463-82">&#91;79&#93;</a></sup> Then they went and told the eleven remaining apostles, who dismissed their story as nonsense.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006227–229_72-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006227–229-72">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESanders1993276–280_73-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESanders1993276–280-73">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECasey2010463_82-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECasey2010463-82">&#91;79&#93;</a></sup> In Luke's account, Jesus never appears to the women,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006227–229_72-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006227–229-72">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESanders1993276–280_73-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESanders1993276–280-73">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECasey2010463–464_83-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECasey2010463–464-83">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup> but instead <a href="/wiki/Road_to_Emmaus_appearance" title="Road to Emmaus appearance">makes his first appearance</a> to <a href="/wiki/Cleopas" title="Cleopas">Cleopas</a> and an unnamed "disciple" on the road to <a href="/wiki/Emmaus" title="Emmaus">Emmaus</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006227–229_72-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006227–229-72">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESanders1993276–280_73-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESanders1993276–280-73">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECasey2010463–464_83-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECasey2010463–464-83">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup> Luke's narrative also removes the injunction for the women to tell the disciples to return to Galilee and instead has Jesus tell the disciples <i>not</i> to return to Galilee, but rather to stay in the precincts of Jerusalem.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECasey2010463–464_83-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECasey2010463–464-83">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006226–227_84-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006226–227-84">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:262px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Alexander_Ivanov_-_Christ%27s_Appearance_to_Mary_Magdalene_after_the_Resurrection_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d8/Alexander_Ivanov_-_Christ%27s_Appearance_to_Mary_Magdalene_after_the_Resurrection_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg/260px-Alexander_Ivanov_-_Christ%27s_Appearance_to_Mary_Magdalene_after_the_Resurrection_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg" decoding="async" width="260" height="196" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d8/Alexander_Ivanov_-_Christ%27s_Appearance_to_Mary_Magdalene_after_the_Resurrection_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg/390px-Alexander_Ivanov_-_Christ%27s_Appearance_to_Mary_Magdalene_after_the_Resurrection_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d8/Alexander_Ivanov_-_Christ%27s_Appearance_to_Mary_Magdalene_after_the_Resurrection_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg/520px-Alexander_Ivanov_-_Christ%27s_Appearance_to_Mary_Magdalene_after_the_Resurrection_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg 2x" data-file-width="4725" data-file-height="3556" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Alexander_Ivanov_-_Christ%27s_Appearance_to_Mary_Magdalene_after_the_Resurrection_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><i>Appearance of Jesus Christ to Maria Magdalena</i> (1835) by <a href="/wiki/Alexander_Andreyevich_Ivanov" title="Alexander Andreyevich Ivanov">Alexander Andreyevich Ivanov</a>. In <a href="https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Bible_(King_James)/John#20:1" class="extiw" title="s:Bible (King James)/John">John 20:1–13</a>, Mary Magdalene sees the risen Jesus alone<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECasey2010464_85-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECasey2010464-85">&#91;82&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006228_81-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006228-81">&#91;78&#93;</a></sup> and he tells her "Don't touch me, for I have not yet ascended to my father."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006228_81-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006228-81">&#91;78&#93;</a></sup></div></div></div>
<p>Mary Magdalene's role in the resurrection narrative is greatly increased in the account from the Gospel of John.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHinkle2003446_79-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHinkle2003446-79">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006253_86-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006253-86">&#91;83&#93;</a></sup> According to <a href="https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Bible_(King_James)/John#20:1" class="extiw" title="s:Bible (King James)/John">John 20:1–10</a>, Mary Magdalene went to the tomb alone when it was still dark and saw that the stone had already been rolled away.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHinkle2003446_79-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHinkle2003446-79">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECasey2010464_85-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECasey2010464-85">&#91;82&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006227,_253_87-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006227,_253-87">&#91;84&#93;</a></sup> She did not see anyone, but immediately ran to tell Peter and the "<a href="/wiki/Disciple_whom_Jesus_loved" title="Disciple whom Jesus loved">beloved disciple</a>",<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHinkle2003446_79-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHinkle2003446-79">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006227,_253_87-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006227,_253-87">&#91;84&#93;</a></sup> who came with her to the tomb and confirmed that it was empty,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHinkle2003446_79-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHinkle2003446-79">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006253_86-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006253-86">&#91;83&#93;</a></sup> but returned home without seeing the risen Jesus.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006253_86-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006253-86">&#91;83&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHinkle2003446_79-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHinkle2003446-79">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup> According to <a href="https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Bible_(King_James)/John#20:11" class="extiw" title="s:Bible (King James)/John">John 20:11–18</a>, Mary, now alone in the garden outside the tomb, saw two angels sitting where Jesus' body had been.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHinkle2003446_79-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHinkle2003446-79">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup> Then the risen Jesus approached her.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHinkle2003446_79-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHinkle2003446-79">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006253,_228_88-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006253,_228-88">&#91;85&#93;</a></sup> She at first mistook him for the gardener,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006253_86-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006253-86">&#91;83&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHinkle2003446_79-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHinkle2003446-79">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup> but, after she heard him say her name, she recognized him and cried out "Rabbouni!" (which is Aramaic for "teacher").<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHinkle2003446_79-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHinkle2003446-79">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006253_86-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006253-86">&#91;83&#93;</a></sup> His next words may be translated as "<a href="/wiki/Noli_me_tangere" title="Noli me tangere">Don't touch me</a>, for I have not yet ascended to my Father" or "Stop clinging to me, [etc.]" the latter more probable in view of the grammar (negated present imperative: stop doing something already in progress) as well as Jesus' challenge to Thomas a week later (see <a href="https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Bible_(King_James)/John#20:24" class="extiw" title="s:Bible (King James)/John">John 20:24–29</a><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECarson1991642_89-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECarson1991642-89">&#91;86&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006228_81-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006228-81">&#91;78&#93;</a></sup>). Jesus then sent her to tell the other apostles the good news of his resurrection.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006253_86-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006253-86">&#91;83&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHinkle2003446_79-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHinkle2003446-79">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup> The Gospel of John therefore portrays Mary Magdalene as the first apostle, the apostle sent <i>to</i> the apostles.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006253_86-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006253-86">&#91;83&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHinkle2003446_79-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHinkle2003446-79">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Because scribes were unsatisfied with the abrupt ending of the Gospel of Mark, they wrote several different alternative endings for it.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECasey2010477_90-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECasey2010477-90">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup> In the "<a href="/wiki/Shorter_ending_of_Mark" class="mw-redirect" title="Shorter ending of Mark">shorter ending</a>", which is found in very few manuscripts, the women go to "those around Peter" and tell them what they had seen at the tomb, followed by a brief declaration of the gospel being preached from east to west.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECasey2010477_90-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECasey2010477-90">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup> This "very forced" ending contradicts the last verse of the original gospel, stating that the women "told no one".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECasey2010477_90-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECasey2010477-90">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup> The "<a href="/wiki/Longer_ending_of_Mark" class="mw-redirect" title="Longer ending of Mark">longer ending</a>", which is found in most surviving manuscripts, is an "amalgam of traditions" containing episodes derived from the other gospels.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECasey2010477_90-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECasey2010477-90">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup> First, it describes an appearance by Jesus to Mary Magdalene alone (as in the Gospel of John),<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECasey2010477_90-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECasey2010477-90">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup> followed by brief descriptions of him appearing to the two disciples on the road to Emmaus (as in the Gospel of Luke) and to the eleven remaining disciples (as in the Gospel of Matthew).<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECasey2010477_90-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECasey2010477-90">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In his book published in 2006, Ehrman states that "it appears virtually certain" that the stories of the empty tomb, regardless of whether or not they are accurate, can definitely be traced back to the historical Mary Magdalene,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006229_91-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006229-91">&#91;88&#93;</a></sup> saying that, in Jewish society, women were regarded as unreliable witnesses and were forbidden from giving testimony in court,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006255_92-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006255-92">&#91;89&#93;</a></sup> so early Christians would have had no motive to make up a story about a woman being the first to discover the empty tomb.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006255_92-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006255-92">&#91;89&#93;</a></sup> In fact, if they had made the story up, they would have had strong motivation to make Peter, Jesus' closest disciple while he was alive, the discoverer of the tomb instead.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006255_92-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006255-92">&#91;89&#93;</a></sup> He also says that the story of Mary Magdalene discovering the empty tomb is independently attested in the Synoptics, the Gospel of John, and in the Gospel of Peter.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006226–227,_255–256_93-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006226–227,_255–256-93">&#91;90&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/N._T._Wright" title="N. T. Wright">N. T. Wright</a> states that "it is, frankly, impossible to imagine that [the women at the tomb] were inserted into the tradition after Paul's day."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWright2003607_94-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWright2003607-94">&#91;91&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECasey2010475_7-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECasey2010475-7">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Casey challenges this argument, contending that the women at the tomb are not legal witnesses, but rather heroines in line with a long Jewish tradition.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECasey2010475_7-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECasey2010475-7">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> He contends that the story of the empty tomb was invented by either the author of the Gospel of Mark or by one of his sources, based on the historically genuine fact that the women really had been present at Jesus' crucifixion and burial.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECasey2010475_7-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECasey2010475-7">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> In his book published in 2014, Ehrman rejects his own previous argument,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2014164–169_95-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2014164–169-95">&#91;92&#93;</a></sup> stating that the story of the empty tomb can only be a later invention because there is virtually no possibility that Jesus' body could have been placed in any kind of tomb<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2014164–169_95-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2014164–169-95">&#91;92&#93;</a></sup> and, if Jesus was never buried, then no one alive at the time could have said that his non-existent tomb had been found empty.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2014164–169_95-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2014164–169-95">&#91;92&#93;</a></sup> He concludes that the idea that early Christians would have had "no motive" to make up the story simply "suffers from a poverty of imagination"<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2014166_96-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2014166-96">&#91;93&#93;</a></sup> and that they would have had all kinds of possible motives,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2014166–169_97-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2014166–169-97">&#91;94&#93;</a></sup> especially since women were overrepresented in early Christian communities and women themselves would have had strong motivation to make up a story about other women being the first to find the tomb.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2014166–167_98-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2014166–167-98">&#91;95&#93;</a></sup> He does conclude later, however, that Mary Magdalene must have been one of the people who had an experience in which she thought she saw the risen Jesus,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2014192_99-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2014192-99">&#91;96&#93;</a></sup> citing her prominence in the gospel resurrection narratives and her absence everywhere else in the gospels as evidence.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2014192_99-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2014192-99">&#91;96&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Apocryphal_early_Christian_writings">Apocryphal early Christian writings</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Mary_Magdalene&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Apocryphal early Christian writings">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/New_Testament_apocrypha" title="New Testament apocrypha">New Testament apocrypha</a></div>
<p>New Testament apocrypha writings mention Mary Magdalene. Some of these writings were cited as <a href="/wiki/Bible" title="Bible">scripture</a> by early Christians. However, they were never admitted to the <a href="/wiki/Development_of_the_New_Testament_canon" title="Development of the New Testament canon">canon of the New Testament</a>. Roman Catholic, Eastern Orthodox and Protestant churches generally do not view these writings as part of the Bible.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2005230_100-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2005230-100">&#91;97&#93;</a></sup> In these apocryphal texts, Mary Magdalene is portrayed as a visionary and leader of the early movement whom Jesus loved more than he loved the other disciples.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKing1998_101-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKing1998-101">&#91;98&#93;</a></sup> These texts were written long after the death of the historical Mary Magdalene.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECasey2010543–544_14-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECasey2010543–544-14">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006247_11-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006247-11">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> They are not regarded by bible scholars as reliable sources of information about her life.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECasey2010543–544_14-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECasey2010543–544-14">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006247_11-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006247-11">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESanders199364_102-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESanders199364-102">&#91;99&#93;</a></sup> Sanders summarizes the scholarly consensus that:
</p>
<blockquote><p>... very, very little in the apocryphal gospels could conceivably go back to the time of Jesus. They are legendary and mythological. Of all the apocryphal material, only some of the sayings in the <a href="/wiki/Gospel_of_Thomas" title="Gospel of Thomas">Gospel of Thomas</a> are worth consideration.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESanders199364_102-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESanders199364-102">&#91;99&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>Nonetheless, the texts have been frequently promoted in modern works as though they were reliable. Such works often support sensationalist statements about Jesus and Mary Magdalene's relationship.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECasey2010544_103-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECasey2010544-103">&#91;100&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Dialogue_of_the_Saviour">Dialogue of the Saviour</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Mary_Magdalene&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: Dialogue of the Saviour">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:172px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Dialogue_of_the_Savior.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/86/Dialogue_of_the_Savior.jpg/170px-Dialogue_of_the_Savior.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="212" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/86/Dialogue_of_the_Savior.jpg/255px-Dialogue_of_the_Savior.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/86/Dialogue_of_the_Savior.jpg/340px-Dialogue_of_the_Savior.jpg 2x" data-file-width="537" data-file-height="670" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Dialogue_of_the_Savior.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Fragment of a fourth-century text of the apocryphal <a href="/wiki/Dialogue_of_the_Saviour" title="Dialogue of the Saviour">Dialogue of the Saviour</a>, in which Mary Magdalene is a central figure<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006207–208_104-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006207–208-104">&#91;101&#93;</a></sup></div></div></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Dialogue_of_the_Saviour" title="Dialogue of the Saviour">Dialogue of the Saviour</a></div>
<p>The earliest dialogue between Jesus and Mary Magdalene is probably the <a href="/wiki/Dialogue_of_the_Saviour" title="Dialogue of the Saviour">Dialogue of the Saviour</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006207_35-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006207-35">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup> a badly damaged Gnostic text discovered in the <a href="/wiki/Nag_Hammadi_library" title="Nag Hammadi library">Nag Hammadi library</a> in 1945.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006207_35-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006207-35">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup> The dialogue consists of a conversation between Jesus, Mary and two apostles - <a href="/wiki/Thomas_the_Apostle" title="Thomas the Apostle">Thomas the Apostle</a> and <a href="/wiki/Matthew_the_Apostle" title="Matthew the Apostle">Matthew the Apostle</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006208_105-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006208-105">&#91;102&#93;</a></sup> In saying 53, the <i>Dialogue</i> attributes to Mary three <a href="/wiki/Aphorism" title="Aphorism">aphorisms</a> that are attributed to Jesus in the New Testament: "The wickedness of each day [is sufficient]. Workers deserve their food. Disciples resemble their teachers."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006208_105-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006208-105">&#91;102&#93;</a></sup> The narrator commends Mary stating "she spoke this utterance as a woman who understood everything."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006208_105-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006208-105">&#91;102&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Pistis_Sophia"><i>Pistis Sophia</i></span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Mary_Magdalene&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Pistis Sophia">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Pistis_Sophia" title="Pistis Sophia">Pistis Sophia</a></div>
<p>The <i><a href="/wiki/Pistis_Sophia" title="Pistis Sophia">Pistis Sophia</a></i>, possibly dating as early as the second century, is the best surviving of the Gnostic writings.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHurtakHurtak1999_106-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHurtakHurtak1999-106">&#91;103&#93;</a></sup> It was discovered in the 18th century in a large volume containing numerous early Gnostic treatises.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006208–209_107-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006208–209-107">&#91;104&#93;</a></sup> The document takes the form of a long dialogue in which Jesus answers his followers' questions.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006209_108-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006209-108">&#91;105&#93;</a></sup> Of the 64 questions, 39 are presented by a woman who is referred to as Mary or Mary Magdalene. At one point, Jesus says, "Mary, thou blessed one, whom I will perfect in all mysteries of those of the height, discourse in openness, thou, whose heart is raised to the kingdom of heaven more than all thy brethren".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHurtakHurtak1999_106-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHurtakHurtak1999-106">&#91;103&#93;</a></sup> At another point, he tells her, "Well done, Mary. You are more blessed than all women on earth, because you will be the fullness of fullness and the completion of completion."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006209_108-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006209-108">&#91;105&#93;</a></sup> Simon Peter, annoyed at Mary's dominance of the conversation, tells Jesus, "My master, we cannot endure this woman who gets in our way and does not let any of us speak, though she talks all the time."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006209_108-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006209-108">&#91;105&#93;</a></sup> Mary defends herself, saying, "My master, I understand in my mind that I can come forward at any time to interpret what Pistis Sophia [a female deity] has said, but I am afraid of Peter, because he threatens me and hates our gender."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006209_108-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006209-108">&#91;105&#93;</a></sup> Jesus assures her, "Any of those filled with the spirit of light will come forward to interpret what I say: no one will be able to oppose them."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006209_108-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006209-108">&#91;105&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Gospel_of_Thomas">Gospel of Thomas</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Mary_Magdalene&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: Gospel of Thomas">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:172px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Nag_Hammadi_coptic.gif" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e6/Nag_Hammadi_coptic.gif/170px-Nag_Hammadi_coptic.gif" decoding="async" width="170" height="244" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/e/e6/Nag_Hammadi_coptic.gif 1.5x" data-file-width="222" data-file-height="318" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Nag_Hammadi_coptic.gif" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Last page of the <a href="/wiki/Gospel_of_Thomas" title="Gospel of Thomas">Gospel of Thomas</a> from Nag Hammadi, containing the account of Jesus' reaffirmation of Mary's authority to Peter<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006211–213_109-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006211–213-109">&#91;106&#93;</a></sup></div></div></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Gospel_of_Thomas" title="Gospel of Thomas">Gospel of Thomas</a></div>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/Gospel_of_Thomas" title="Gospel of Thomas">Gospel of Thomas</a>, usually dated to the late first or early second century, was among the ancient texts discovered in the <a href="/wiki/Nag_Hammadi_library" title="Nag Hammadi library">Nag Hammadi library</a> in 1945.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMeyer1992_110-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMeyer1992-110">&#91;107&#93;</a></sup> The Gospel of Thomas consists entirely of 114 sayings attributed to Jesus.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006210–211_111-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006210–211-111">&#91;108&#93;</a></sup> Many of these sayings are similar to ones in the canonical gospels,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006210_112-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006210-112">&#91;109&#93;</a></sup> but others are completely unlike anything found in the New Testament.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006210–211_111-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006210–211-111">&#91;108&#93;</a></sup> Some scholars believe that at least a few of these sayings may authentically be traced back to the historical Jesus.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006210_112-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006210-112">&#91;109&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESanders199364_102-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESanders199364-102">&#91;99&#93;</a></sup> Two of the sayings reference a woman named "Mary", who is generally regarded as Mary Magdalene.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006210–211_111-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006210–211-111">&#91;108&#93;</a></sup> In saying 21, Mary herself asks Jesus the perfectly innocuous question, "Whom are your disciples like?"<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006211_113-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006211-113">&#91;110&#93;</a></sup> Jesus responds, "They are like children who have settled in a field which is not theirs. When the owners of the field come, they will say, 'Let us have back our field.' They (will) undress in their presence in order to let them have back their field and to give it back to them". Following this, Jesus continues his explanation with a parable about the owner of a house and a thief, ending with the common rhetoric, "Whoever has ears to hear let him hear".
</p><p>Mary's mention in saying 114, however, has generated considerable controversy:<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006211_113-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006211-113">&#91;110&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"/><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>Simon Peter said to them: Let Mary go forth from among us, for women are not worthy of the life. Jesus said: Behold, I shall lead her, that I may make her male, in order that she also may become a living spirit like you males.
