For every woman who makes herself male shall enter into the kingdom of heaven.</p><div class="templatequotecite">—&#8201;<cite><a href="#CITEREFMeyer1992">Meyer 1992</a></cite></div></blockquote>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Gospel_of_Philip">Gospel of Philip</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Mary_Magdalene&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: Gospel of Philip">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:172px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Evangelio_de_Felipe_%C2%B7_Codex_II,_3_%C2%B7_Biblioteca_Copta_de_Nag_Hammadi.png" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/df/Evangelio_de_Felipe_%C2%B7_Codex_II%2C_3_%C2%B7_Biblioteca_Copta_de_Nag_Hammadi.png/170px-Evangelio_de_Felipe_%C2%B7_Codex_II%2C_3_%C2%B7_Biblioteca_Copta_de_Nag_Hammadi.png" decoding="async" width="170" height="322" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/df/Evangelio_de_Felipe_%C2%B7_Codex_II%2C_3_%C2%B7_Biblioteca_Copta_de_Nag_Hammadi.png/255px-Evangelio_de_Felipe_%C2%B7_Codex_II%2C_3_%C2%B7_Biblioteca_Copta_de_Nag_Hammadi.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/df/Evangelio_de_Felipe_%C2%B7_Codex_II%2C_3_%C2%B7_Biblioteca_Copta_de_Nag_Hammadi.png/340px-Evangelio_de_Felipe_%C2%B7_Codex_II%2C_3_%C2%B7_Biblioteca_Copta_de_Nag_Hammadi.png 2x" data-file-width="354" data-file-height="670" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Evangelio_de_Felipe_%C2%B7_Codex_II,_3_%C2%B7_Biblioteca_Copta_de_Nag_Hammadi.png" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Text of the <a href="/wiki/Gospel_of_Philip" title="Gospel of Philip">Gospel of Philip</a> from <a href="/wiki/Nag_Hammadi" title="Nag Hammadi">Nag Hammadi</a></div></div></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Gospel_of_Philip" title="Gospel of Philip">Gospel of Philip</a></div>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/Gospel_of_Philip" title="Gospel of Philip">Gospel of Philip</a>, dating from the second or third century, survives in part among the texts found in Nag Hammadi in 1945.<sup id="cite_ref-OT&amp;NT_114-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-OT&amp;NT-114">&#91;d&#93;</a></sup> In a manner very similar to <a href="https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Bible_(King_James)/John#19:25" class="extiw" title="s:Bible (King James)/John">John 19:25–26</a>, the Gospel of Philip presents Mary Magdalene among Jesus' female entourage, adding that she was his <i>koinônos</i>,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006215_115-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006215-115">&#91;111&#93;</a></sup> a Greek word variously translated in contemporary versions as partner, associate, comrade, companion:<sup id="cite_ref-116" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-116">&#91;112&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006215_115-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006215-115">&#91;111&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"/><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>There were three who always walked with the Lord: Mary, his mother, and <a href="/wiki/Mary_of_Clopas" title="Mary of Clopas">her sister</a>, and Magdalene, who was called his companion. His sister,<sup id="cite_ref-confusing_117-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-confusing-117">&#91;e&#93;</a></sup> his mother and his companion were each a Mary.</p><div class="templatequotecite">—&#8201;<cite><a href="#CITEREFGrant1961">Grant 1961</a>, pp.&#160;129–140</cite></div></blockquote>
<p>The Gospel of Philip uses cognates of <i>koinônos</i> and Coptic equivalents to refer to the literal pairing of men and women in marriage and sexual intercourse, but also metaphorically, referring to a spiritual partnership, and the reunification of the Gnostic Christian with the divine realm.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMarjanen1996151–160_118-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarjanen1996151–160-118">&#91;113&#93;</a></sup> The Gospel of Philip also contains another passage relating to Jesus' relationship with Mary Magdalene.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006215_115-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006215-115">&#91;111&#93;</a></sup> The text is badly fragmented, and speculated but unreliable additions are shown in brackets:
</p>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"/><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>And the companion of the [saviour was] Mary Magdalene. [Christ] loved Mary more than [all] the disciples, [and used to] kiss her [often] on the [mouth]. The rest of the disciples [were offended by it and expressed disapproval]. They said to him, "Why do you love her more than all of us?" The Saviour answered and said to them, "Why do I not love you like her? When a blind man and one who sees are both together in darkness, they are no different from one another. When the light comes, then he who sees will see the light, and he who is blind will remain in darkness."<sup id="cite_ref-GospelOfPhilip_119-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-GospelOfPhilip-119">&#91;114&#93;</a></sup></p><div class="templatequotecite">—&#8201;<cite><a href="#CITEREFGrant1961">Grant 1961</a>, pp.&#160;129–140</cite></div></blockquote>
<p>For early Christians, kissing did not have a romantic connotation and it was common for Christians to kiss their fellow believers as a way of greeting.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDickson200695_120-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDickson200695-120">&#91;115&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006216_121-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006216-121">&#91;116&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-122" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-122">&#91;f&#93;</a></sup> This tradition is still practiced in many Christian congregations today and is known as the "<a href="/wiki/Kiss_of_peace" title="Kiss of peace">kiss of peace</a>".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006215_115-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006215-115">&#91;111&#93;</a></sup> Ehrman explains that, in the context of the Gospel of Philip, the kiss of peace is used as a symbol for the passage of truth from one person to another<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006215–216_123-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006215–216-123">&#91;117&#93;</a></sup> and that it is not in any way an act of "divine foreplay".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006216_121-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006216-121">&#91;116&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Gospel_of_Mary">Gospel of Mary</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Mary_Magdalene&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: Gospel of Mary">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:172px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Gospel_of_Mary.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/49/Gospel_of_Mary.jpg/170px-Gospel_of_Mary.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="183" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/49/Gospel_of_Mary.jpg/255px-Gospel_of_Mary.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/49/Gospel_of_Mary.jpg/340px-Gospel_of_Mary.jpg 2x" data-file-width="669" data-file-height="720" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Gospel_of_Mary.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><a href="/wiki/Papyrus_Oxyrhynchus_L_3525" title="Papyrus Oxyrhynchus L 3525">Papyrus Oxyrhynchus L 3525</a>, a fragment of the Greek text of the <a href="/wiki/Gospel_of_Mary" title="Gospel of Mary">Gospel of Mary</a></div></div></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Gospel_of_Mary" title="Gospel of Mary">Gospel of Mary</a></div>
<p>The Gospel of Mary is the only surviving apocryphal text named after a woman.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006238_124-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006238-124">&#91;118&#93;</a></sup> It contains information about the role of women in the early church.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006239_125-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006239-125">&#91;119&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-GOM_126-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-GOM-126">&#91;120&#93;</a></sup> The text was probably written over a century after the historical Mary Magdalene's death.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006247_11-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006247-11">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> The text is not attributed to her and its author is anonymous.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006247_11-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006247-11">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> Instead, it received its title because it is <i>about</i> her.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006247_11-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006247-11">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> The main surviving text comes from a Coptic translation preserved in a fifth-century manuscript (<i>Berolinensis Gnosticus</i> 8052,1) discovered in <a href="/wiki/Cairo" title="Cairo">Cairo</a> in 1896.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006238–249_127-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006238–249-127">&#91;121&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECasey2010535_128-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECasey2010535-128">&#91;122&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-GOM_126-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-GOM-126">&#91;120&#93;</a></sup> As a result of numerous intervening conflicts, the manuscript was not published until 1955.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006238_124-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006238-124">&#91;118&#93;</a></sup> Roughly half the text of the gospel in this manuscript has been <a href="/wiki/Lost_works" class="mw-redirect" title="Lost works">lost</a>;<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006249_129-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006249-129">&#91;123&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEde_Boer200552_130-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEde_Boer200552-130">&#91;124&#93;</a></sup> the first six pages and four from the middle are missing.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006249_129-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006249-129">&#91;123&#93;</a></sup> In addition to this Coptic translation, two brief third-century fragments of the gospel in the original Greek (<a href="/wiki/Papyrus_Rylands_463" title="Papyrus Rylands 463">P. Rylands 463</a> and <a href="/wiki/Papyrus_Oxyrhynchus_L_3525" title="Papyrus Oxyrhynchus L 3525">P. Oxyrhynchus 3525</a>) have also been discovered, which were published in 1938 and 1983 respectively.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECasey2010535_128-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECasey2010535-128">&#91;122&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-GOM_126-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-GOM-126">&#91;120&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The first part of the gospel deals with Jesus' parting words to his followers after a post-resurrection appearance.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006239–242_131-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006239–242-131">&#91;125&#93;</a></sup> Mary first appears in the second part, in which she tells the other disciples, who are all in fright for their own lives: "Do not weep or grieve or be in doubt, for his grace will be with you all and will protect you. Rather, let us praise his greatness, for he has prepared us and made us truly human."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006242_132-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006242-132">&#91;126&#93;</a></sup> Unlike in the Gospel of Thomas, where women can only be saved by becoming men, in the Gospel of Mary, they can be saved just as they are.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006242–243_133-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006242–243-133">&#91;127&#93;</a></sup> Peter approaches Mary and asks her:
</p>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"/><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>"Sister we know that the Savior loved you more than the rest of woman. Tell us the words of the Savior which you remember which you know, but we do not, nor have we heard them". Mary answered and said, "What is hidden from you I will proclaim to you". And she began to speak to them these words: "I", she said, "I saw the Lord in a vision and I said to Him, Lord I saw you today in a vision".</p><div class="templatequotecite">—&#8201;<cite><a href="#CITEREFde_Boer2005">de Boer 2005</a>, p.&#160;74</cite></div></blockquote>
<p>Mary then proceeds to describe the <a href="/wiki/Gnosticism" title="Gnosticism">Gnostic</a> cosmology in depth, revealing that she is the only one who has understood Jesus' true teachings.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006243–245_134-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006243–245-134">&#91;128&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECasey2010535–536_135-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECasey2010535–536-135">&#91;129&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Andrew_the_Apostle" title="Andrew the Apostle">Andrew the Apostle</a> challenges Mary, insisting, "Say what you think about what she said, but I do not believe the savior said this. These teachings are strange ideas."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006245_136-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006245-136">&#91;130&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECasey2010536_137-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECasey2010536-137">&#91;131&#93;</a></sup> Peter responds, saying, "Did he really speak with a woman in private, without our knowledge? Should we all listen to her? Did he prefer her to us?"<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006245_136-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006245-136">&#91;130&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECasey2010536_137-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECasey2010536-137">&#91;131&#93;</a></sup> Andrew and Peter's responses are intended to demonstrate that they do not understand Jesus' teachings<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006245_136-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006245-136">&#91;130&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECasey2010536_137-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECasey2010536-137">&#91;131&#93;</a></sup> and that it is really only Mary who truly understands.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006245–246_138-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006245–246-138">&#91;132&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECasey2010536_137-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECasey2010536-137">&#91;131&#93;</a></sup> Matthew the Apostle comes to Mary's defense, giving a sharp rebuke to Peter:<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006245_136-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006245-136">&#91;130&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECasey2010536_137-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECasey2010536-137">&#91;131&#93;</a></sup> "Peter, you are always angry. Now I see you arguing against this woman like an adversary. If the savior made her worthy, who are you to reject her? Surely the savior knows her well. That is why he loved her more than us."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006246_139-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006246-139">&#91;133&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECasey2010536_137-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECasey2010536-137">&#91;131&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Borborite_scriptures">Borborite scriptures</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Mary_Magdalene&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: Borborite scriptures">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/Borborites" title="Borborites">Borborites</a>, also known as the Phibionites, were an early Christian <a href="/wiki/Gnosticism" title="Gnosticism">Gnostic</a> sect during the late fourth century who had numerous scriptures involving Mary Magdalene,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKim201537–39_140-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKim201537–39-140">&#91;134&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDeConick2011139_141-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDeConick2011139-141">&#91;135&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEStrongStrong200890_142-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEStrongStrong200890-142">&#91;136&#93;</a></sup> including <i>The Questions of Mary</i>, <i>The Greater Questions of Mary</i>, <i>The Lesser Questions of Mary</i>, and <i>The Birth of Mary</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKim201537–39_140-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKim201537–39-140">&#91;134&#93;</a></sup> None of these texts have survived to the present,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKim201537–39_140-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKim201537–39-140">&#91;134&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEStrongStrong200890_142-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEStrongStrong200890-142">&#91;136&#93;</a></sup> but they are mentioned by the early Christian heretic-hunter <a href="/wiki/Epiphanius_of_Salamis" title="Epiphanius of Salamis">Epiphanius of Salamis</a> in his <i><a href="/wiki/Panarion" title="Panarion">Panarion</a></i>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKim201537–39_140-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKim201537–39-140">&#91;134&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006235_143-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006235-143">&#91;137&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEStrongStrong200890_142-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEStrongStrong200890-142">&#91;136&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Epiphanius_144-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Epiphanius-144">&#91;138&#93;</a></sup> Epiphanius says that the <i>Greater Questions of Mary</i> contained an episode in which, during a post-resurrection appearance, Jesus took Mary to the top of a mountain, where he pulled a woman out of his side and engaged in sexual intercourse with her.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006235_143-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006235-143">&#91;137&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Epiphanius_144-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Epiphanius-144">&#91;138&#93;</a></sup> Then, upon <a href="/wiki/Ejaculation" title="Ejaculation">ejaculating</a>, Jesus <a href="/wiki/Seminophagia" class="mw-redirect" title="Seminophagia">drank his own semen</a> and told Mary, "Thus we must do, that we may live."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006235_143-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006235-143">&#91;137&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDeConick2011139_141-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDeConick2011139-141">&#91;135&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Epiphanius_144-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Epiphanius-144">&#91;138&#93;</a></sup> Upon hearing this, Mary instantly fainted, to which Jesus responded by helping her up and telling her, "O thou of little faith, wherefore didst thou doubt?"<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006235_143-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006235-143">&#91;137&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDeConick2011139_141-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDeConick2011139-141">&#91;135&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Epiphanius_144-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Epiphanius-144">&#91;138&#93;</a></sup> This story was supposedly the basis for the Borborite <a href="/wiki/Eucharist" title="Eucharist">Eucharist</a> ritual in which they allegedly engaged in orgies and drank semen and menstrual blood as the "body and blood of Christ" respectively.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006234–235_145-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006234–235-145">&#91;139&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDeConick2011139_141-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDeConick2011139-141">&#91;135&#93;</a></sup> Ehrman casts doubt on the accuracy of Epiphanius's summary, commenting that "the details of Epiphanius's description sound very much like what you can find in the ancient rumor mill about secret societies in the ancient world".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006235_143-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006235-143">&#91;137&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Legacy">Legacy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Mary_Magdalene&amp;action=edit&amp;section=12" title="Edit section: Legacy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Patristic_era">Patristic era</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Mary_Magdalene&amp;action=edit&amp;section=13" title="Edit section: Patristic era">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:262px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Dura_Europos_baptistry_women_at_the_tomb.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c1/Dura_Europos_baptistry_women_at_the_tomb.jpg/260px-Dura_Europos_baptistry_women_at_the_tomb.jpg" decoding="async" width="260" height="123" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c1/Dura_Europos_baptistry_women_at_the_tomb.jpg/390px-Dura_Europos_baptistry_women_at_the_tomb.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c1/Dura_Europos_baptistry_women_at_the_tomb.jpg/520px-Dura_Europos_baptistry_women_at_the_tomb.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1173" data-file-height="556" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Dura_Europos_baptistry_women_at_the_tomb.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>This fresco from the <a href="/wiki/Nave" title="Nave">nave</a> of the <a href="/wiki/Dura-Europos_church" title="Dura-Europos church">Dura-Europos church</a> dates to <abbr title="circa">c.</abbr> 240<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHaskins200559_146-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHaskins200559-146">&#91;140&#93;</a></sup> and contains the oldest surviving depiction of Mary Magdalene.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHaskins200558–59_147-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHaskins200558–59-147">&#91;141&#93;</a></sup> She is shown alongside two other women (the third now almost completely missing due to extensive damage),<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHaskins200559_146-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHaskins200559-146">&#91;140&#93;</a></sup> each holding a lit torch and a bowl of <a href="/wiki/Myrrh" title="Myrrh">myrrh</a>, as they approach <a href="/wiki/Tomb_of_Jesus" title="Tomb of Jesus">Jesus' tomb</a>, which is still sealed.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHaskins200558–61_148-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHaskins200558–61-148">&#91;142&#93;</a></sup></div></div></div>
<p>Most of the earliest <a href="/wiki/Church_Fathers" title="Church Fathers">Church Fathers</a> do not mention Mary Magdalene at all,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESchaberg200486_149-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESchaberg200486-149">&#91;143&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006185_12-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006185-12">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHaskins200590_150-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHaskins200590-150">&#91;144&#93;</a></sup> and those who do mention her usually only discuss her very briefly.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESchaberg200486_149-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESchaberg200486-149">&#91;143&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006185_12-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006185-12">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHaskins200590_150-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHaskins200590-150">&#91;144&#93;</a></sup> In his anti-Christian polemic <i><a href="/wiki/The_True_Word" title="The True Word">The True Word</a></i>, written between 170 and 180, the pagan philosopher <a href="/wiki/Celsus" title="Celsus">Celsus</a> declared that Mary Magdalene was nothing more than "a hysterical female... who either dreamt in a certain state of mind and through wishful thinking had a hallucination due to some mistaken notion (an experience which has happened to thousands), or, which is more likely, wanted to impress others by telling this fantastic tale, and so by this cock-and-bull story to provide a chance for other beggars."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESchaberg200484–85_151-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESchaberg200484–85-151">&#91;145&#93;</a></sup> The Church Father <a href="/wiki/Origen" title="Origen">Origen</a> (<abbr title="circa">c.</abbr> 184 – <abbr title="circa">c.</abbr> 253) defended Christianity against this accusation in his apologetic treatise <i><a href="/wiki/Against_Celsus" class="mw-redirect" title="Against Celsus">Against Celsus</a></i>, mentioning <a href="https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Bible_(King_James)/Matthew#28:1" class="extiw" title="s:Bible (King James)/Matthew">Matthew 28:1</a>, which lists Mary Magdalene and "the other Mary" both seeing the resurrected Jesus, thus providing a second witness.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESchaberg200485_152-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESchaberg200485-152">&#91;146&#93;</a></sup> Origen also preserves a statement from Celsus that some Christians in his day followed the teachings of a woman named "Mariamme", who is almost certainly Mary Magdalene.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESchaberg200487_153-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESchaberg200487-153">&#91;147&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006256_154-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006256-154">&#91;148&#93;</a></sup> Origen merely dismisses this, remarking that Celsus "pours on us a heap of names".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESchaberg200487_153-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESchaberg200487-153">&#91;147&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Mary Magdalene has the reputation in <a href="/wiki/Western_Christianity" title="Western Christianity">Western Christianity</a> as being a repentant prostitute or loose woman; however, these statements are not supported by the canonical gospels, which at no point imply that she had ever been a prostitute or in any way notable for a sinful way of life.<sup id="cite_ref-BBC_2-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-BBC-2">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDoyle2011_155-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDoyle2011-155">&#91;149&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006189–190_156-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006189–190-156">&#91;150&#93;</a></sup> The misconception likely arose due to a conflation between Mary Magdalene, <a href="/wiki/Mary_of_Bethany" title="Mary of Bethany">Mary of Bethany</a> (who <a href="/wiki/Anointing_of_Jesus" title="Anointing of Jesus">anoints Jesus' feet</a> in <a href="https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Bible_(King_James)/John#11:1" class="extiw" title="s:Bible (King James)/John">John 11:1–12</a>), and the unnamed "sinful woman" who anoints Jesus' feet in <a href="https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Bible_(King_James)/Luke#7:36" class="extiw" title="s:Bible (King James)/Luke">Luke 7:36–50</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-BBC_2-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-BBC-2">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDoyle2011_155-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDoyle2011-155">&#91;149&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMorrow1999_157-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMorrow1999-157">&#91;151&#93;</a></sup> As early as the third century, the Church Father <a href="/wiki/Tertullian" title="Tertullian">Tertullian</a> (<abbr title="circa">c.</abbr> 160 – 225) references the touch of "the woman which was a sinner" in effort to prove that Jesus "was not a phantom, but really a solid body."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESchaberg200485_152-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESchaberg200485-152">&#91;146&#93;</a></sup> This may indicate that Mary Magdalene was already being conflated with the "sinful woman" in <a href="https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Bible_(King_James)/Luke#7:36" class="extiw" title="s:Bible (King James)/Luke">Luke 7:36–50</a>, though Tertullian never clearly identifies the woman of whom he speaks as Mary Magdalene.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESchaberg200485_152-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESchaberg200485-152">&#91;146&#93;</a></sup> A sermon attributed to <a href="/wiki/Hippolytus_of_Rome" title="Hippolytus of Rome">Hippolytus of Rome</a> (<abbr title="circa">c.</abbr> 170 – 235) refers to Mary of Bethany and her sister <a href="/wiki/Martha" title="Martha">Martha</a> seeking Jesus in the garden like Mary Magdalene in <a href="https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Bible_(King_James)/John#20:1" class="extiw" title="s:Bible (King James)/John">John 20</a>, indicating a conflation between Mary of Bethany and Mary Magdalene.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESchaberg200485–86_158-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESchaberg200485–86-158">&#91;152&#93;</a></sup> The sermon describes the conflated woman as a "second <a href="/wiki/Eve" title="Eve">Eve</a>" who compensates for the disobedience of the first Eve through her obedience.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESchaberg200486_149-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESchaberg200486-149">&#91;143&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHaskins200590_150-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHaskins200590-150">&#91;144&#93;</a></sup> The sermon also explicitly identifies Mary Magdalene and the other women as "apostles".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006253_86-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006253-86">&#91;83&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBock2004143–144_159-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBock2004143–144-159">&#91;153&#93;</a></sup> The first clear identification of Mary Magdalene as a redeemed sinner comes from <a href="/wiki/Ephrem_the_Syrian" title="Ephrem the Syrian">Ephrem the Syrian</a> (<abbr title="circa">c.</abbr> 306 – 373).<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHooper200581_160-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHooper200581-160">&#91;154&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAlthaus-Reid200986_161-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAlthaus-Reid200986-161">&#91;155&#93;</a></sup> Part of the reason for the identification of Mary Magdalene as a sinner may derive from the reputation of her birthplace, Magdala,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHaskins200515_162-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHaskins200515-162">&#91;156&#93;</a></sup> which, by the late first century, was infamous for its inhabitants' alleged vice and licentiousness.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHaskins200515_162-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHaskins200515-162">&#91;156&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In one of his preserved sayings, <a href="/wiki/Gregory_of_Nyssa" title="Gregory of Nyssa">Gregory of Nyssa</a> (<abbr title="circa">c.</abbr> 330 – 395) identifies Mary Magdalene as "the first witness to the resurrection, that she might set straight again by her faith in the resurrection, what was turned over in her transgression."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESchaberg200486–87_163-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESchaberg200486–87-163">&#91;157&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Ambrose" title="Ambrose">Ambrose</a> (<abbr title="circa">c.</abbr> 340 – 397), by contrast, not only rejected the conflation of Mary Magdalene, Mary of Bethany, and the anointing sinner,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMaisch199844_164-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMaisch199844-164">&#91;158&#93;</a></sup> but even proposed that the authentic Mary Magdalene herself was, in fact, two separate people:<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMaisch199844_164-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMaisch199844-164">&#91;158&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHaskins200593_165-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHaskins200593-165">&#91;159&#93;</a></sup> one woman named Mary Magdalene who discovered the empty tomb and a different Mary Magdalene who saw the risen Christ.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMaisch199844_164-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMaisch199844-164">&#91;158&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Augustine_of_Hippo" title="Augustine of Hippo">Augustine of Hippo</a> (354 – 430) entertained the possibility that Mary of Bethany and the unnamed sinner from Luke might be the same person,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHaskins200593–94_166-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHaskins200593–94-166">&#91;160&#93;</a></sup> but did not associate Mary Magdalene with either of them.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHaskins200594_167-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHaskins200594-167">&#91;161&#93;</a></sup> Instead, Augustine praised Mary Magdalene as "unquestionably... surpassingly more ardent in her love than these other women who had administered to the Lord".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHaskins200594_167-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHaskins200594-167">&#91;161&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_Middle_Ages">Early Middle Ages</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Mary_Magdalene&amp;action=edit&amp;section=14" title="Edit section: Early Middle Ages">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1096954695/mw-parser-output/.tmulti">.mw-parser-output .tmulti .multiimageinner{display:flex;flex-direction:column}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .trow{display:flex;flex-direction:row;clear:left;flex-wrap:wrap;width:100%;box-sizing:border-box}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .tsingle{margin:1px;float:left}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .theader{clear:both;font-weight:bold;text-align:center;align-self:center;background-color:transparent;width:100%}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .thumbcaption{background-color:transparent}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .text-align-left{text-align:left}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .text-align-right{text-align:right}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .text-align-center{text-align:center}@media all and (max-width:720px){.mw-parser-output .tmulti .thumbinner{width:100%!important;box-sizing:border-box;max-width:none!important;align-items:center}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .trow{justify-content:center}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .tsingle{float:none!important;max-width:100%!important;box-sizing:border-box;text-align:center}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .tsingle .thumbcaption{text-align:left}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .trow>.thumbcaption{text-align:center}}</style><div class="thumb tmulti tright"><div class="thumbinner multiimageinner" style="width:352px;max-width:352px"><div class="trow"><div class="tsingle" style="width:182px;max-width:182px"><div class="thumbimage"><a href="/wiki/File:Tilman_Riemenschneider_Magdalena-3.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4f/Tilman_Riemenschneider_Magdalena-3.jpg/180px-Tilman_Riemenschneider_Magdalena-3.jpg" decoding="async" width="180" height="270" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4f/Tilman_Riemenschneider_Magdalena-3.jpg/270px-Tilman_Riemenschneider_Magdalena-3.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4f/Tilman_Riemenschneider_Magdalena-3.jpg/360px-Tilman_Riemenschneider_Magdalena-3.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2432" data-file-height="3648" /></a></div><div class="thumbcaption"><i>Ascension of Mary Magdalene</i> by <a href="/wiki/Tilman_Riemenschneider" title="Tilman Riemenschneider">Tilman Riemenschneider</a> (1490–92)<br />A depiction of Mary Magdalene with <a href="/wiki/Feather_tights#Mary_Magdalene&#39;s_hair_suit" title="Feather tights">thick body hair</a></div></div><div class="tsingle" style="width:166px;max-width:166px"><div class="thumbimage"><a href="/wiki/File:Carlo_Crivelli_-_Maria_Magdalena_001_(Cropped).jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0f/Carlo_Crivelli_-_Maria_Magdalena_001_%28Cropped%29.jpg/164px-Carlo_Crivelli_-_Maria_Magdalena_001_%28Cropped%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="164" height="267" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0f/Carlo_Crivelli_-_Maria_Magdalena_001_%28Cropped%29.jpg/246px-Carlo_Crivelli_-_Maria_Magdalena_001_%28Cropped%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0f/Carlo_Crivelli_-_Maria_Magdalena_001_%28Cropped%29.jpg/328px-Carlo_Crivelli_-_Maria_Magdalena_001_%28Cropped%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="753" data-file-height="1228" /></a></div><div class="thumbcaption"><i>Mary Magdalene</i> (<abbr title="circa">c.</abbr> 1480–1487), altarpiece in <a href="/wiki/International_Gothic" title="International Gothic">International Gothic</a> style by <a href="/wiki/Carlo_Crivelli" title="Carlo Crivelli">Carlo Crivelli</a> showing her with long, blonde hair</div></div></div></div></div>
<p>The unnamed "sinful woman" in <a href="https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Bible_(King_James)/Luke#7:36" class="extiw" title="s:Bible (King James)/Luke">Luke 7:36–50</a> is never identified as a prostitute<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006189_168-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006189-168">&#91;162&#93;</a></sup> and, in Jewish society at the time the gospel was written, "sinful" could have simply meant that she "did not assiduously observe the <a href="/wiki/Law_of_Moses" title="Law of Moses">law of Moses</a>".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006189_168-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006189-168">&#91;162&#93;</a></sup> The notion of Mary Magdalene specifically being a former prostitute or loose woman dates to a narrative in an influential <a href="/wiki/Homily" title="Homily">homily</a> by <a href="/wiki/Pope_Gregory_I" title="Pope Gregory I">Pope Gregory I</a> ("Gregory the Great") in around 591,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHooper200581_160-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHooper200581-160">&#91;154&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECarroll2006_169-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECarroll2006-169">&#91;163&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006189–190_156-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006189–190-156">&#91;150&#93;</a></sup> in which he not only identifies Magdalene with the anonymous sinner with the perfume in Luke's gospel and with Mary of Bethany, the sister of Martha and <a href="/wiki/Lazarus_of_Bethany" title="Lazarus of Bethany">Lazarus</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006189–190_156-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006189–190-156">&#91;150&#93;</a></sup> but also, for the first time, explicitly identifies her sins as ones of a sexual nature:<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006189–190_156-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006189–190-156">&#91;150&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"/><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>She whom Luke calls the sinful woman, whom John calls Mary, we believe to be the Mary from whom seven devils were ejected according to Mark. What did these seven devils signify, if not all the vices? It is clear, that the woman previously used the unguent to perfume her flesh in forbidden acts. What she therefore displayed more scandalously, she was now offering to God in a more praiseworthy manner. She had coveted with earthly eyes, but now through penitence these are consumed with tears. She displayed her hair to set off her face, but now her hair dries her tears. She had spoken proud things with her mouth, but in kissing the Lord's feet, she now planted her mouth on the Redeemer's feet. For every delight, therefore, she had had in herself, she now immolated herself. She turned the mass of her crimes to virtues, in order to serve God entirely in penance.</p><div class="templatequotecite">—&#8201;<cite>Pope Gregory I (<i>homily XXXIII</i>), <a href="#CITEREFCarroll2006">Carroll 2006</a></cite></div></blockquote>
<p>In Pope Gregory's interpretation, the seven demons expelled from Mary Magdalene by Jesus are transformed into the <a href="/wiki/Seven_deadly_sins" title="Seven deadly sins">seven deadly sins</a> of medieval Catholicism,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMorrow1999_157-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMorrow1999-157">&#91;151&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHaskins200514_170-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHaskins200514-170">&#91;164&#93;</a></sup> leading Mary "to be condemned not only for lust, but for pride and covetousness as well."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMorrow1999_157-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMorrow1999-157">&#91;151&#93;</a></sup> The aspect of the repentant sinner became almost equally significant as the disciple in her <i>persona</i> as depicted in Western art and religious literature, fitting well with the great importance of <a href="/wiki/Penitence" class="mw-redirect" title="Penitence">penitence</a> in medieval theology. In subsequent religious legend, Mary's story became conflated with that of <a href="/wiki/Mary_of_Egypt" title="Mary of Egypt">Mary of Egypt</a>, a repentant prostitute who then lived as a hermit. With that, Mary's image was, according to Susan Haskins, author of <i>Mary Magdalene: Myth and Metaphor,</i> "finally settled...for nearly fourteen hundred years,"<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHaskins200595_171-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHaskins200595-171">&#91;165&#93;</a></sup> although in fact the most important late medieval popular accounts of her life describe her as a rich woman whose life of sexual freedom is purely for pleasure.<sup id="cite_ref-172" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-172">&#91;166&#93;</a></sup> This composite depiction of Mary Magdalene was carried into the Mass texts for her feast day: in the <a href="/wiki/Tridentine_Mass" title="Tridentine Mass">Tridentine Mass</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Collect" title="Collect">collect</a> explicitly identifies her as Mary of Bethany by describing Lazarus as her brother, and the Gospel is the story of the penitent woman anointing Jesus' feet.<sup id="cite_ref-173" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-173">&#91;167&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The "composite Magdalene" was never accepted by the <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox" class="mw-redirect" title="Eastern Orthodox">Eastern Orthodox</a> churches, who saw only Mary the disciple, and believed that after the Resurrection she lived as a companion to Mary the mother of Jesus, and not even in the West was it universally accepted. The <a href="/wiki/Benedictine" class="mw-redirect" title="Benedictine">Benedictine</a> Order always celebrated Mary of Bethany together with Martha and Lazarus of Bethany on July 29, while Mary Magdalene was celebrated on July 22.<sup id="cite_ref-174" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-174">&#91;168&#93;</a></sup> Not only John Chrysostom in the East (<i>Matthew, Homily 88</i>), but also Ambrose (<i>De virginitate</i> 3,14; 4,15) in the West, when speaking of Mary Magdalene after the resurrection of Jesus Christ, far from calling her a harlot, suggest she was a virgin.<sup id="cite_ref-Hufstader_175-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Hufstader-175">&#91;169&#93;</a></sup> Starting in around the eighth century, Christian sources record mention of a church in Magdala purported to have been built on the site of Mary Magdalene's house, where Jesus exorcized her of the seven demons.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPringle199828_176-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPringle199828-176">&#91;170&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In an eastern tradition supported by the western bishop and historian <a href="/wiki/Gregory_of_Tours" title="Gregory of Tours">Gregory of Tours</a> (<abbr title="circa">c.</abbr> 538 – 594), Mary Magdalene is said to have retired to <a href="/wiki/Ephesus" title="Ephesus">Ephesus</a> in <a href="/wiki/Asia_Minor" class="mw-redirect" title="Asia Minor">Asia Minor</a> with Mary the mother of Jesus, where they both lived out the rests of their lives.<sup id="cite_ref-177" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-177">&#91;171&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFoss197933_178-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFoss197933-178">&#91;172&#93;</a></sup> Gregory states that Mary Magdalene was buried in the city of Ephesus.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFoss197933_178-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFoss197933-178">&#91;172&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Modestus_of_Jerusalem" title="Modestus of Jerusalem">Modestus</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Greek_Orthodox_Patriarch_of_Jerusalem" title="Greek Orthodox Patriarch of Jerusalem">Patriarch of Jerusalem</a> from 630 until 634, describes a slightly different tradition that Mary Magdalene had come to Ephesus to live with the apostle John following the death of Mary the mother of Jesus.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFoss197933_178-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFoss197933-178">&#91;172&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="High_Middle_Ages">High Middle Ages</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Mary_Magdalene&amp;action=edit&amp;section=15" title="Edit section: High Middle Ages">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Fictional_biographies">Fictional biographies</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Mary_Magdalene&amp;action=edit&amp;section=16" title="Edit section: Fictional biographies">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>Starting in early <a href="/wiki/High_Middle_Ages" title="High Middle Ages">High Middle Ages</a>, writers in western Europe began developing elaborate fictional biographies of Mary Magdalene's life, in which they heavily embellished upon the vague details given in the gospels.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMaisch199846_179-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMaisch199846-179">&#91;173&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006183–184_180-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006183–184-180">&#91;174&#93;</a></sup> Stories about <a href="/wiki/Nobility" title="Nobility">noble</a> saints were popular during this time period;<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMaisch199846_179-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMaisch199846-179">&#91;173&#93;</a></sup> accordingly, tales of Mary Magdalene's wealth and social status became heavily exaggerated.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMaisch199846–47_181-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMaisch199846–47-181">&#91;175&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006183–184_180-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006183–184-180">&#91;174&#93;</a></sup> In the tenth century, <a href="/wiki/Odo_of_Cluny" title="Odo of Cluny">Odo of Cluny</a> (<abbr title="circa">c.</abbr> 880 – 942) wrote a sermon in which he described Mary as an extraordinarily wealthy noblewoman of royal descent.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMaisch199846–49_182-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMaisch199846–49-182">&#91;176&#93;</a></sup> Some manuscripts of the sermon record that Mary's parents were named Syrus and Eucharia<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMaisch199847_183-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMaisch199847-183">&#91;177&#93;</a></sup> and one manuscript goes into great detail describing her family's purported land holdings in Bethany, Jerusalem, and Magdala.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMaisch199847_183-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMaisch199847-183">&#91;177&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The theologian <a href="/wiki/Honorius_Augustodunensis" title="Honorius Augustodunensis">Honorius Augustodunensis</a> (<abbr title="circa">c.</abbr> 1080 – <abbr title="circa">c.</abbr> 1151) embellished this tale even further, reporting that Mary was a wealthy noblewoman who was married in "Magdalum",<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMaisch199847_183-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMaisch199847-183">&#91;177&#93;</a></sup> but that she committed adultery, so she fled to Jerusalem and became a "public sinner" (<i>vulgaris meretrix</i>).<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMaisch199847_183-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMaisch199847-183">&#91;177&#93;</a></sup> Honorius mentions that, out of love for Jesus, Mary repented and withdrew into a life of quiet isolation.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMaisch199847_183-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMaisch199847-183">&#91;177&#93;</a></sup> Under the influence of stories about other female saints, such as <a href="/wiki/Mary_of_Egypt" title="Mary of Egypt">Mary of Egypt</a> and <a href="/wiki/Pelagia" title="Pelagia">Pelagia</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMaisch199847_183-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMaisch199847-183">&#91;177&#93;</a></sup> painters in Italy during the ninth and tenth centuries gradually began to develop the image of Mary Magdalene living alone in the desert as a penitent <a href="/wiki/Asceticism" title="Asceticism">ascetic</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMaisch199847_183-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMaisch199847-183">&#91;177&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMormando1999257–274_184-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMormando1999257–274-184">&#91;178&#93;</a></sup> This portrayal became so popular that it quickly spread to Germany and England.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMaisch199847_183-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMaisch199847-183">&#91;177&#93;</a></sup> From the twelfth century, Abbot Hugh of Semur (died 1109), Peter Abelard (died 1142), and Geoffrey of Vendome (died 1132) all referred to Mary Magdalene as the sinner who merited the title <i>apostolorum apostola</i> (Apostle to the Apostles), with the title becoming commonplace during the twelfth and thirteenth centuries.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESchaberg200488_185-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESchaberg200488-185">&#91;179&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Alleged_burial_in_France">Alleged burial in France</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Mary_Magdalene&amp;action=edit&amp;section=17" title="Edit section: Alleged burial in France">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>In western Europe, elaborate and conflicting legends began to develop, which said that Mary Magdalene had travelled to southern France and died there.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWitcombe2002279_186-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWitcombe2002279-186">&#91;180&#93;</a></sup> Starting in around 1050, the monks of the <a href="/wiki/Abbey_of_la_Madaleine,_V%C3%A9zelay" class="mw-redirect" title="Abbey of la Madaleine, Vézelay">Abbey of la Madaleine, Vézelay</a> in Burgundy said they discovered Mary Magdalene's actual skeleton.<sup id="cite_ref-187" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-187">&#91;181&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMaisch199848_188-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMaisch199848-188">&#91;182&#93;</a></sup> At first, the existence of the skeleton was merely asserted,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMaisch199848_188-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMaisch199848-188">&#91;182&#93;</a></sup> but, in 1265, the monks made a spectacular, public show of "discovering" it<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMaisch199848_188-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMaisch199848-188">&#91;182&#93;</a></sup> and, in 1267, the bones were brought before the king of France himself, who venerated them.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMaisch199848_188-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMaisch199848-188">&#91;182&#93;</a></sup> On December 9, 1279, an excavation ordered by <a href="/wiki/Charles_II_of_Naples" title="Charles II of Naples">Charles II, King of Naples</a> at <a href="/wiki/Saint-Maximin-la-Sainte-Baume" title="Saint-Maximin-la-Sainte-Baume">Saint-Maximin-la-Sainte-Baume</a>, Provence, led to the discovery of another purported burial of Mary Magdalene.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMcCarthy201050_189-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcCarthy201050-189">&#91;183&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMaisch199848_188-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMaisch199848-188">&#91;182&#93;</a></sup> The shrine was purportedly found intact, with an explanatory inscription stating why the relics had been hidden.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHaskins2005129–132_190-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHaskins2005129–132-190">&#91;184&#93;</a></sup> Charles II commissioned the building of a new <a href="/wiki/Gothic_architecture" title="Gothic architecture">Gothic</a> <a href="/wiki/Basilica" title="Basilica">basilica</a> on the site and, in return for providing accommodation for <a href="/wiki/Pilgrim" title="Pilgrim">pilgrims</a>, the town's residents were exempt from taxes.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDavidsonGitlitz2002562_191-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDavidsonGitlitz2002562-191">&#91;185&#93;</a></sup> Saint-Maximin-la-Sainte-Baume gradually displaced <a href="/wiki/V%C3%A9zelay" title="Vézelay">Vézelay</a> in popularity and acceptance.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHaskins2005129–132_190-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHaskins2005129–132-190">&#91;184&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:172px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Copiebasiliquereliquaire.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/82/Copiebasiliquereliquaire.jpg/170px-Copiebasiliquereliquaire.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="264" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/82/Copiebasiliquereliquaire.jpg/255px-Copiebasiliquereliquaire.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/82/Copiebasiliquereliquaire.jpg/340px-Copiebasiliquereliquaire.jpg 2x" data-file-width="400" data-file-height="621" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Copiebasiliquereliquaire.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>In 1279, the monks of <a href="/wiki/Saint-Maximin-la-Sainte-Baume" title="Saint-Maximin-la-Sainte-Baume">Saint-Maximin-la-Sainte-Baume</a> said they discovered Mary Magdalene's skeleton.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMcCarthy201050_189-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcCarthy201050-189">&#91;183&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMaisch199848_188-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMaisch199848-188">&#91;182&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Reliquary" title="Reliquary">reliquary</a> at St. Maximin, created in the nineteenth century, contains her purported skull.</div></div></div>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:172px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Torun_SS_Johns_Mary_Magdalene.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/41/Torun_SS_Johns_Mary_Magdalene.jpg/170px-Torun_SS_Johns_Mary_Magdalene.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="227" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/41/Torun_SS_Johns_Mary_Magdalene.jpg/255px-Torun_SS_Johns_Mary_Magdalene.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/41/Torun_SS_Johns_Mary_Magdalene.jpg/340px-Torun_SS_Johns_Mary_Magdalene.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1379" data-file-height="1838" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Torun_SS_Johns_Mary_Magdalene.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><a href="/wiki/International_Gothic" title="International Gothic">International Gothic</a> <i>Elevation of Mary Magdalene</i> with angels raising her in <a href="/wiki/Cathedral_Basilica_of_St._John_the_Baptist_and_St._John_the_Evangelist,_Toru%C5%84" class="mw-redirect" title="Cathedral Basilica of St. John the Baptist and St. John the Evangelist, Toruń">SS. Johns' Cathedral</a> in <a href="/wiki/Toru%C5%84" title="Toruń">Toruń</a></div></div></div>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="The_Golden_Legend">The Golden Legend</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Mary_Magdalene&amp;action=edit&amp;section=18" title="Edit section: The Golden Legend">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>The most famous account of Mary Magdalene's legendary life comes from <i><a href="/wiki/Golden_Legend" title="Golden Legend">The Golden Legend</a></i>, a collection of medieval saints stories compiled in around the year 1260 by the Italian writer and <a href="/wiki/Dominican_Order" title="Dominican Order">Dominican friar</a> <a href="/wiki/Jacobus_de_Voragine" title="Jacobus de Voragine">Jacobus de Voragine</a> (<abbr title="circa">c.</abbr> 1230 – 1298).<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006184_192-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006184-192">&#91;186&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMaisch199848_188-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMaisch199848-188">&#91;182&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEErhardtMorris20127_193-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEErhardtMorris20127-193">&#91;187&#93;</a></sup> In this account, Mary Magdalene is, in Ehrman's words, "fabulously rich, insanely beautiful, and outrageously sensual",<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006184_192-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006184-192">&#91;186&#93;</a></sup> but she gives up her life of wealth and sin to become a devoted follower of Jesus.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006184_192-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006184-192">&#91;186&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEErhardtMorris20127–8_194-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEErhardtMorris20127–8-194">&#91;188&#93;</a></sup> Fourteen years after Jesus' crucifixion, some pagans throw Mary, Martha, <a href="/wiki/Lazarus_of_Bethany" title="Lazarus of Bethany">Lazarus</a> (who, in this account, is their brother due to a conflation with Mary of Bethany), and two other Christians named <a href="/wiki/Maximinus_of_Aix" title="Maximinus of Aix">Maximin</a> and Cedonius onto a rudderless boat in the <a href="/wiki/Mediterranean_Sea" title="Mediterranean Sea">Mediterranean Sea</a> to die.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006184_192-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006184-192">&#91;186&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEErhardtMorris20127_193-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEErhardtMorris20127-193">&#91;187&#93;</a></sup> Miraculously, however, the boat washes ashore at <a href="/wiki/Marseille" title="Marseille">Marseille</a> in southern France.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006184_192-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006184-192">&#91;186&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEErhardtMorris20127_193-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEErhardtMorris20127-193">&#91;187&#93;</a></sup> Mary persuades the governor of the city not to offer sacrifices to a pagan god<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006184_192-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006184-192">&#91;186&#93;</a></sup> and later persuades him to convert to Christianity after she proves the Christian God's power by successfully praying to Him to make the governor's wife pregnant.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006184_192-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006184-192">&#91;186&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEErhardtMorris20127_193-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEErhardtMorris20127-193">&#91;187&#93;</a></sup> The governor and his wife sail for Rome to meet the apostle Peter in person,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006184_192-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006184-192">&#91;186&#93;</a></sup> but their ship is struck by a storm, which causes the wife to go into labor.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006184_192-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006184-192">&#91;186&#93;</a></sup> The wife dies in childbirth and the governor leaves her on an island with the still-living infant at her breast.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006184_192-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006184-192">&#91;186&#93;</a></sup> The governor spends two years with Peter in Rome<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006184_192-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006184-192">&#91;186&#93;</a></sup> and, on his way home, he stops at the same island to discover that, due to Mary Magdalene's miraculous long-distance intercession, his child has survived for two years on his dead mother's breast milk.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006184–185_195-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006184–185-195">&#91;189&#93;</a></sup> Then the governor's wife rises from the dead and tells him that Mary Magdalene has brought her back.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006185_12-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006185-12">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> The whole family returns to Marseille, where they meet Mary again in person.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006185_12-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006185-12">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> Mary herself spends the last thirty years of her life alone as a penitent ascetic in a cave in a desert in the French region of <a href="/wiki/Provence" title="Provence">Provence</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEErhardtMorris20127_193-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEErhardtMorris20127-193">&#91;187&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHead2001659_196-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHead2001659-196">&#91;190&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESaxer1959_197-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESaxer1959-197">&#91;191&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-198" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-198">&#91;192&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEJansen2001172_199-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEJansen2001172-199">&#91;193&#93;</a></sup> At every <a href="/wiki/Canonical_hours" title="Canonical hours">canonical hour</a>, the angels come and lift her up to hear their songs in Heaven.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEErhardtMorris20127_193-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEErhardtMorris20127-193">&#91;187&#93;</a></sup> On the last day of her life, Maximin, now the bishop of <a href="/wiki/Aix-en-Provence" title="Aix-en-Provence">Aix</a>, comes to her and gives her the Eucharist.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEErhardtMorris20127_193-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEErhardtMorris20127-193">&#91;187&#93;</a></sup> Mary cries tears of joy<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEErhardtMorris20127_193-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEErhardtMorris20127-193">&#91;187&#93;</a></sup> and, after taking it, she lies down and dies.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEErhardtMorris20127_193-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEErhardtMorris20127-193">&#91;187&#93;</a></sup> De Voragine gives the common account of the transfer of Mary Magdalene's relics from her sepulchre in the <a href="/wiki/Oratory_(worship)" title="Oratory (worship)">oratory</a> of Saint Maximin at <a href="/wiki/Aix-en-Provence" title="Aix-en-Provence">Aix-en-Provence</a> to the newly founded <a href="/wiki/V%C3%A9zelay" title="Vézelay">Vézelay</a>;<sup id="cite_ref-200" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-200">&#91;194&#93;</a></sup> the transportation of the relics is entered as undertaken in 771 by the founder of the abbey, identified as Gerard, <a href="/wiki/Duke_of_Burgundy" title="Duke of Burgundy">Duke of Burgundy</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-GoldenLegend_201-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-GoldenLegend-201">&#91;195&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Spouse_of_John_the_Evangelist">Spouse of John the Evangelist</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Mary_Magdalene&amp;action=edit&amp;section=19" title="Edit section: Spouse of John the Evangelist">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>The monk and historian <a href="/wiki/Domenico_Cavalca" title="Domenico Cavalca">Domenico Cavalca</a> (<abbr title="circa">c.</abbr> 1270 – 1342), citing <a href="/wiki/Jerome" title="Jerome">Jerome</a>, suggested that Mary Magdalene was <a href="/wiki/Engagement" title="Engagement">betrothed</a> to <a href="/wiki/John_the_Evangelist" title="John the Evangelist">John the Evangelist</a>: "I like to think that the Magdalene was the spouse of John, not affirming it... I am glad and blythe that St Jerome should say so".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEJansen2001151,_footnote_20_citing_Cavalca,_&#39;&#39;Vita&#39;&#39;,_329;_&#39;&#39;Life&#39;&#39;,_2–3._202-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEJansen2001151,_footnote_20_citing_Cavalca,_&#39;&#39;Vita&#39;&#39;,_329;_&#39;&#39;Life&#39;&#39;,_2–3.-202">&#91;196&#93;</a></sup> They were sometimes thought to be the couple at the <a href="/wiki/Wedding_at_Cana" title="Wedding at Cana">Wedding at Cana</a>, though the Gospel accounts say nothing of the ceremony being abandoned. In the <i>Golden Legend</i>, De Voragine dismisses talk of John and Mary being betrothed and John leaving his bride at the altar to follow Jesus as nonsense.<sup id="cite_ref-GoldenLegend_201-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-GoldenLegend-201">&#91;195&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Late_Middle_Ages_and_Renaissance">Late Middle Ages and Renaissance</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Mary_Magdalene&amp;action=edit&amp;section=20" title="Edit section: Late Middle Ages and Renaissance">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1096954695/mw-parser-output/.tmulti"/><div class="thumb tmulti tright"><div class="thumbinner multiimageinner" style="width:408px;max-width:408px"><div class="trow"><div class="tsingle" style="width:202px;max-width:202px"><div class="thumbimage"><a href="/wiki/File:Donatello,_maria_maddalena_02.JPG" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e1/Donatello%2C_maria_maddalena_02.JPG/200px-Donatello%2C_maria_maddalena_02.JPG" decoding="async" width="200" height="267" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e1/Donatello%2C_maria_maddalena_02.JPG/300px-Donatello%2C_maria_maddalena_02.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e1/Donatello%2C_maria_maddalena_02.JPG/400px-Donatello%2C_maria_maddalena_02.JPG 2x" data-file-width="1536" data-file-height="2048" /></a></div><div class="thumbcaption"><i><a href="/wiki/Penitent_Magdalene_(Donatello)" title="Penitent Magdalene (Donatello)">Penitent Magdalene</a></i> (<abbr title="circa">c.</abbr> 1454) by <a href="/wiki/Donatello" title="Donatello">Donatello</a>, showing her as "an old, emaciated and toothless woman... worn down by years of hard solitude in her cave".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHaag2016231_203-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHaag2016231-203">&#91;197&#93;</a></sup> The sculpture is an "extreme" example of Mary Magdalene's usual portrayal as a penitent ascetic.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKing2012188_204-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKing2012188-204">&#91;198&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHaag2016231_203-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHaag2016231-203">&#91;197&#93;</a></sup></div></div><div class="tsingle" style="width:202px;max-width:202px"><div class="thumbimage"><a href="/wiki/File:Giampetrino-Leonardo.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b6/Giampetrino-Leonardo.jpg/200px-Giampetrino-Leonardo.jpg" decoding="async" width="200" height="270" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b6/Giampetrino-Leonardo.jpg/300px-Giampetrino-Leonardo.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b6/Giampetrino-Leonardo.jpg/400px-Giampetrino-Leonardo.jpg 2x" data-file-width="800" data-file-height="1080" /></a></div><div class="thumbcaption"><i>Mary Magdalene</i> (<abbr title="circa">c.</abbr> 1515), traditionally attributed to <a href="/wiki/Leonardo_da_Vinci" title="Leonardo da Vinci">Leonardo da Vinci</a>'s student <a href="/wiki/Giampietrino" title="Giampietrino">Giampietrino</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHaag2016235–336_205-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHaag2016235–336-205">&#91;199&#93;</a></sup> This painting shows a very different image of Mary Magdalene as "a woman who repents of nothing, who feels no shame or guilt."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHaag2016337_206-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHaag2016337-206">&#91;200&#93;</a></sup></div></div></div></div></div>
<p>The thirteenth-century <a href="/wiki/Cistercian" class="mw-redirect" title="Cistercian">Cistercian</a> monk and chronicler <a href="/wiki/Peter_of_Vaux_de_Cernay" class="mw-redirect" title="Peter of Vaux de Cernay">Peter of Vaux de Cernay</a> said it was part of <a href="/wiki/Catharism" title="Catharism">Catharist</a> belief that the earthly Jesus Christ had a relationship with Mary Magdalene, described as his <a href="/wiki/Concubinage" title="Concubinage">concubine</a>: "Further, in their secret meetings they said that the Christ who was born in the earthly and visible Bethlehem and crucified at Jerusalem was "evil", and that Mary Magdalene was his concubine – and that she was the woman taken in adultery who is referred to in the Scriptures."<sup id="cite_ref-207" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-207">&#91;201&#93;</a></sup> A document, possibly written by Ermengaud of <a href="/wiki/B%C3%A9ziers" title="Béziers">Béziers</a>, undated and anonymous and attached to his <i>Treatise against Heretics</i>,<sup id="cite_ref-208" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-208">&#91;202&#93;</a></sup> makes a similar statement:<sup id="cite_ref-209" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-209">&#91;203&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"/><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>Also they [the Cathars] teach in their secret meetings that Mary Magdalene was the wife of Christ. She was the Samaritan woman to whom He said, "Call thy husband". She was the woman taken into adultery, whom Christ set free lest the Jews stone her, and she was with Him in three places, in the temple, at the well, and in the garden. After the Resurrection, He appeared first to her.<sup id="cite_ref-210" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-210">&#91;204&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>In the middle of the fourteenth century, a Dominican friar wrote a biography of Mary Magdalene in which he described her brutally mutilating herself after giving up prostitution,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKing2012188_204-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKing2012188-204">&#91;198&#93;</a></sup> clawing at her legs until they bled, tearing out clumps of her hair, and beating her face with her fists and her breasts with stones.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKing2012188_204-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKing2012188-204">&#91;198&#93;</a></sup> This portrayal of her inspired the sculptor <a href="/wiki/Donatello" title="Donatello">Donatello</a> (<abbr title="circa">c.</abbr> 1386 – 1466) to portray her as a gaunt and beaten ascetic in his wooden sculpture <i><a href="/wiki/Penitent_Magdalene_(Donatello)" title="Penitent Magdalene (Donatello)">Penitent Magdalene</a></i> (<abbr title="circa">c.</abbr> 1454) for the <a href="/wiki/Florence_Baptistery" title="Florence Baptistery">Florence Baptistery</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKing2012188_204-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKing2012188-204">&#91;198&#93;</a></sup> In 1449, <a href="/wiki/Ren%C3%A9_of_Anjou" title="René of Anjou">King René d'Anjou</a> gave to <a href="/wiki/Angers_Cathedral" title="Angers Cathedral">Angers Cathedral</a> the <a href="/wiki/Amphora" title="Amphora">amphora</a> from <a href="/wiki/Cana" title="Cana">Cana</a> in which Jesus changed water to wine, acquiring it from the nuns of Marseilles, who told him that Mary Magdalene had brought it with her from Judea, relating to the legend where she was the jilted bride at the wedding after which John the Evangelist received his calling from Jesus.<sup id="cite_ref-211" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-211">&#91;g&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Reformation_and_Counter-Reformation">Reformation and Counter-Reformation</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Mary_Magdalene&amp;action=edit&amp;section=21" title="Edit section: Reformation and Counter-Reformation">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:242px;"><a href="/wiki/File:RubensSimonCyreneCarriesCross.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/ce/RubensSimonCyreneCarriesCross.jpg/240px-RubensSimonCyreneCarriesCross.jpg" decoding="async" width="240" height="271" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/c/ce/RubensSimonCyreneCarriesCross.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="266" data-file-height="300" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:RubensSimonCyreneCarriesCross.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><i><a href="/wiki/Christ_and_the_Penitent_Sinners" title="Christ and the Penitent Sinners">Christ and the Penitent Sinners</a></i> (1617) by <a href="/wiki/Peter_Paul_Rubens" title="Peter Paul Rubens">Peter Paul Rubens</a> is a typical example of how Mary Magdalene was portrayed during the <a href="/wiki/Baroque" title="Baroque">Baroque</a> era, emphasizing her erotic allure and blurring the lines between religious and <a href="/wiki/Erotic_art" title="Erotic art">erotic art</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMaisch199863–65_212-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMaisch199863–65-212">&#91;205&#93;</a></sup></div></div></div>
<p>In 1517, on the brink of the <a href="/wiki/Protestant_Reformation" class="mw-redirect" title="Protestant Reformation">Protestant Reformation</a>, the leading French <a href="/wiki/Renaissance_humanist" class="mw-redirect" title="Renaissance humanist">Renaissance humanist</a> <a href="/wiki/Jacques_Lef%C3%A8vre_d%27%C3%89taples" title="Jacques Lefèvre d&#39;Étaples">Jacques Lefèvre d'Étaples</a> published his book <i>De Maria Magdalena et triduo Christi disceptatio</i> (<i>Disputation on Mary Magdalene and the Three Days of Christ</i>), in which he argued against the conflation of Mary Magdalene, Mary of Bethany, and the unnamed sinner in Luke.<sup id="cite_ref-Hufstader_175-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Hufstader-175">&#91;169&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHaskins2005250_213-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHaskins2005250-213">&#91;206&#93;</a></sup> Various authors published a flurry of books and pamphlets in response, the vast majority of which opposed Lefèvre d'Étaples.<sup id="cite_ref-Hufstader_175-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Hufstader-175">&#91;169&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHaskins2005250–251_214-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHaskins2005250–251-214">&#91;207&#93;</a></sup> In 1521, the theology faculty of the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Paris" title="University of Paris">Sorbonne</a> formally condemned the idea that the three women were separate people as heretical,<sup id="cite_ref-Hufstader_175-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Hufstader-175">&#91;169&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHaskins2005250–251_214-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHaskins2005250–251-214">&#91;207&#93;</a></sup> and debate died down, overtaken by the larger issues raised by <a href="/wiki/Martin_Luther" title="Martin Luther">Martin Luther</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Hufstader_175-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Hufstader-175">&#91;169&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHaskins2005250–251_214-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHaskins2005250–251-214">&#91;207&#93;</a></sup> Luther and <a href="/wiki/Huldrych_Zwingli" title="Huldrych Zwingli">Huldrych Zwingli</a> (1484 – 1531) both supported the composite Magdalene.<sup id="cite_ref-Henderson_2004,_pp._8–14_215-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Henderson_2004,_pp._8–14-215">&#91;208&#93;</a></sup> Luther, whose views on sexuality were much more liberal than those of his fellow reformers,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERoper2016295–296_216-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERoper2016295–296-216">&#91;209&#93;</a></sup> reportedly once joked to a group of friends that "even pious Christ himself" had committed adultery three times: once with Mary Magdalene, once with the <a href="/wiki/Samaritan_woman_at_the_well" title="Samaritan woman at the well">Samaritan woman at the well</a>, and once with <a href="/wiki/Jesus_and_the_woman_taken_in_adultery" title="Jesus and the woman taken in adultery">the adulteress he had let off so easily</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERoper2016295_217-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERoper2016295-217">&#91;210&#93;</a></sup> Because the cult of Mary Magdalene was inextricably associated with the Catholic teaching of the <a href="/wiki/Intercession_of_saints" title="Intercession of saints">intercession of saints</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHaskins2005249_218-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHaskins2005249-218">&#91;211&#93;</a></sup> it came under particularly harsh criticism by Protestant leaders.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHaskins2005249_218-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHaskins2005249-218">&#91;211&#93;</a></sup> Zwingli demanded for the cult of Mary Magdalene to be abolished and all images of her to be destroyed.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHaskins2005249_218-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHaskins2005249-218">&#91;211&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/John_Calvin" title="John Calvin">John Calvin</a> (1509 – 1564) not only rejected the composite Magdalene,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHaskins2005249_218-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHaskins2005249-218">&#91;211&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Henderson_2004,_pp._8–14_215-1" class="reference"><a
